« Mon amour pour ce sport reste intact » : Poussé dehors par Haas, Esteban Ocon publie un message d'adieu très classe sur Instagram

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Mis à la porte par l'écurie Haas avec effet immédiat, Esteban Ocon a pris la parole ce mardi 10 novembre 2026. Le pilote français a tenu à adresser un message sobre à ses supporters ainsi qu'à l'ensemble du paddock de la Formule 1.

Cette prise de parole intervient une semaine après l'officialisation de son éviction, actée le mardi 3 novembre 2026 pour les quatre derniers Grands Prix de la saison 2026. Une rupture brutale alors qu'Esteban Ocon, 30 ans, occupait la 16e place du classement des pilotes avec 7 points, contre 20 points pour son coéquipier Oliver Bearman.

Sur son compte Instagram, le vainqueur du Grand Prix de Hongrie 2021 a publié un texte sobre, sans la moindre rancœur envers sa direction. Esteban Ocon a préféré remercier chaleureusement les personnes qui l'ont soutenu durant cette épreuve, rappelant son attachement indéfectible pour sa discipline.

Le pilote tricolore compte 199 engagements et 198 départs en Formule 1, ainsi que 4 podiums en carrière.

L'aventure s'est pourtant terminée dans la tourmente. En effet, tout s'est accéléré le samedi 31 octobre 2026 lors du Grand Prix de Mexico sur le circuit de l'Autódromo Hermanos Rodríguez. Mis à l'écart soudainement après un différend interne lors des essais libres du vendredi, le Français avait alors cédé sa place au réserviste japonais Ryo Hirakawa. Un coup dur.

Une séparation était déjà programmée fin septembre 2026, mais l'accord prévoyait initialement qu'il termine la saison. Toutefois, le directeur de l'écurie, Ayao Komatsu, a affirmé avoir pris cette décision anticipée dans le meilleur intérêt de la structure. Haas a d'ailleurs aligné le rookie brésilien Rafael Câmara, déjà engagé pour 2027, dès le Grand Prix de São Paulo le week-end du 8 novembre 2026.

Désormais sur la touche, Esteban Ocon fait face à un futur incertain. À trois manches de la fin de la saison 2026, il ne dispose d'aucun baquet titulaire confirmé pour 2027. Au-delà des doutes contractuels, sa passion et son amour pour la Formule 1 restent aussi forts que jamais.

Le prochain rendez-vous du championnat du monde de Formule 1 2026 sera le Grand Prix de Las Vegas, 21e manche de la saison, programmé du 19 au 21 novembre 2026 sur le Las Vegas Strip Circuit.

Mercato - Ça bouge à l’OM : Qui doit être le prochain joueur à partir cet été ?

Fa il politico: «Il voltafaccia di assoallenatori e assocalciatori da Malagò a Galliani? A me non crea nessun imbarazzo, ma loro hanno fatto una brutta figura». Cyril Martin-Pichon : "Content d'affronter les Verts !"

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Ancien capitaine de la réserve stéphanoise, Cyril Martin-Pichon s'est confié à Poteaux Carrés avant d'affronter son club formateur ce dimanche après-midi à Annecy lors du 7e tour de Coupe de France. Dans cette première partie de l'entretien, le milieu de terrain du GFA Rumilly-Vallières revient sur ses vertes années.

La Piche, comment as-tu vécu ce tirage au sort ? Tu souhaitais tomber sur Sainté ? Tu pressentais que tu allais retrouver les Verts ? Je suis super content de ce tirage. Super content ! Quand j’ai vu les groupes, je me suis dit : « pourquoi pas tomber sur Sainté ? » Pour moi c’était le meilleur tirage ! Peut-être que dans l'absolu ça aurait été mieux de jouer une équipe plus petite pour avoir plus de chances de continuer. Mais non en fait, je suis content. En vrai, tout le monde était content du tirage. Ça va être une belle fête !Tu sens qu’il y a une certaine excitation au sein de ton club et de son environnement à quelques jours de cette affiche ? Oui, c'est sûr. On va jouer contre les leaders de Ligue 2, et au-delà de ça, les Verts c'est un club mythique. Mais on a un match de championnat à jouer ce mercredi contre Saint-Priest avant ce match de Coupe de France. Chaque chose en son temps. L’affiche de dimanche, c’est beaucoup de plaisir, ça a amené du sourire à beaucoup de personnes, surtout des bénévoles qui sont supporters des Verts. Le président aussi, ça fait un moment qu'il nous demande d'essayer de faire un parcours en Coupe pour taper un gros. Tout le monde est content ! On a un ou deux membres du staff qui sont supporters des Verts. Je pense notamment à notre analyste vidéo, Arnaud Morel. Le président du club supporte également les Verts. Son fils aussi. Parmi les bénévoles, je vais citer un gars qui s’appelle Pino, c’est un vrai supporter des Verts. Bruno Piccon, c’est pareil. Il connaît bien la famille Chambost. Et puis il y a moi quelque part ! Bon, on ne va pas se mentir, je ne suis pas un fervent supporter mais évidemment les Verts ne me laissent pas insensible. L’ASSE, c’est mon club formateur, j’y ai passé plusieurs années. Je suis forcément les résultats et je regarde les résumés des matches. Et puis mon père est supporter des Verts. A l’époque où Léo et Dylan étaient encore au club, je regardais un peu plus les matches, j’ai trouvé ça top qu’ils contribuent tous les deux à faire monter les Verts en L1. A un niveau plus modeste, on avait connu ensemble les joies d’une montée avec la réserve 6 ans auparavant. Tu as défendu nos couleurs 4 ans et demi, de juillet 2015 à janvier 2020. Que gardes-tu de cette expérience ? Franchement, que du positif ! Que ce soit la formation, le passage dans le monde adulte ou même avec les pros. J’ai fait de belles rencontres. Je ne suis pas du tout revanchard comme certains le sont.Tu as joué sous les ordres de Lionel Vaillant et Gilles Rodriguez en U17, sous la houlette de Jean-Philippe Primard puis Julien Sablé en U19, sous la direction de Laurent Batlles puis Razik Nedder en équipe réserve. Lequel de ces entraîneurs t’aura le plus marqué ?Sans hésiter, Laurent Batlles. De loin. Je pense que c'est l’entraîneur qui m'a fait le plus progresser. C’est avec lui que j’ai entretenu les meilleures relations, que le courant passait le mieux. C'est avec lui aussi qu'on a eu les meilleurs résultats, j'ai envie de dire. Même si je n’oublie pas qu’en U19 avec Jean-Philippe Primard, on a fait une très belle saison [en 2015-2016, ndp2]. On avait fini en tête de notre groupe après avoir battu l’OM 4-0 à Marseille. Mais en demi-finale, on s’était incliné sur le même score à la maison contre une grosse équipe du PSG. Je me souviens qu’en face il y avait Ikoné, Nkunku, Jean-Kévin Augustin, Odsonne Edouard. Paris avait une sacrée attaque !Mais j’ai passé deux années vraiment top quand Laurent Batlles entraînait la réserve. La première année, on est monté en N2 en finissant premiers du groupe. Et la seconde on s’est maintenu sans trembler. C’est la période où j’ai pris le plus de plaisir dans le jeu. J’ai beaucoup appris sous les ordres du coach Batlles. Il m’a notamment fait progresser dans mon agressivité, que ce soit avec le ballon ou sans ballon. J’ai passé un cap avec lui. J’ai pris du plaisir dans son équipe. J'avais peut-être un peu plus de maturité dans mon jeu, vu que c'est la première fois que je découvrais le monde adulte.Tu auras beaucoup moins joué avec Razik Nedder. Après quelques mois avec lui tu as quitté le club à mi-saison pour retourner à Bourgoin, ta ville natale. Tu sentais que ton avenir était bouché à Sainté ? J’ai signé pro quand on est monté de N3 à N2. J’avais repris avec les pros, fait la prépa avec eux. J’avais joué les matches amicaux de l’équipe première, je me souviens qu’on avait battu Lens, Marseille, Nice… Mais ensuite j’ai joué la saison avec la réserve. Ensuite, Laurent Batlles est parti à Troyes et c’est Razik qui a récupéré la réserve tandis que Ghislain Printant a succédé à Jean-Louis Gasset à la tête de l’équipe première. Je me suis entraîné un peu avec les pros et j’ai joué 4 mois avec la réserve, j’ai même porté le brassard de capitaine quelques matches. Mais la volonté du club était de faire monter pas mal de jeunes qui avaient gagné la Gambardella quelques mois plus tôt. Razik et le staff de Puel m’ont fait comprendre qu’il valait mieux que je trouve un autre projet… J’aurais pu rester car j’avais encore 18 mois de contrat. Mais moi j’avais besoin de jouer et de retrouver du plaisir. Qu'est-ce qui t'a manqué à ton avis pour t’imposer chez les pros ? Il y avait des joueurs devant moi qui étaient plus que confirmés. Difficile de rivaliser avec un Yann M’Vila par exemple. Mahdi Camara montait en puissance. Ce n’était pas évident de se mettre à leur niveau. Après, j'aurais pu, je pense, mentalement, un peu plus m'accrocher. Je pense que mentalement, je n'étais pas encore prêt. Si j’avais eu un peu plus la dalle, j’aurais peut-être pu gratter des choses.Quel est le meilleur joueur que tu aies côtoyé à Sainté ?Au centre de formation, j’ai côtoyé beaucoup de bons joueurs mais il y en a un que j'aime particulièrement parce qu’on parle le même football, c'est Dylan Chambost. J'ai toujours aimé ce profil de joueur. Il a un pied gauche exceptionnel. Et puis, il est beau à voir jouer. Il a la classe, je trouve. Dans un registre différent, j’aime beaucoup Mahdi Camara. Je l'ai vu évoluer. D'une saison à une autre, il a pris en maturité, en confiance en lui. C’est un joueur qui a toujours eu la dalle. A un moment donné il a passé un cap techniquement et dans la maturité aussi. Et vraiment, à ce moment-là, il m'a impressionné. Après, je citerais Allan Saint-Maximin. Lui, c'était un phénomène. Et sur la fin, j’ai comme tout le monde été impressionné par William Saliba. Il était surclassé avec nous. Chez les pros, le joueur qui m’aura le plus marqué est Yann M’Vila. Même en dehors du terrain, c’était la classe. Il était hyper gentil, de bon conseil et bienveillant avec les jeunes. Sur le rectangle vert, il m’a impressionné par sa qualité de passe, sa lecture du jeu, son placement. Il était toujours bien placé, ne perdait pas un ballon, en récupérait beaucoup. Je le trouvais très fort.Quels étaient tes meilleurs potes au centre de formation, ceux dont tu étais le plus proche et que tu côtoyais le plus en dehors des terrains ? Les deux années où j’ai joué avec eux en réserve dans l’équipe entraînée par Laurent Batlles, j’étais proche de Léo Pétrot, Dylan Chambost et Mickaël Panos. J’étais beaucoup avec Adrien Fleury aussi. Après je m'entendais super bien avec Mickaël Nadé, Ronaël Pierre-Gabriel. Je continue de suivre le beau parcours de tout ce beau monde mais de loin, forcément ! On s’envoie à l’occasion des petits messages. A l’époque de Laurent Batlles, on avait vraiment une belle équipe réserve. Je me réjouis de la réussite de mes anciens coéquipiers. Quand tu vois que Mahdi a brillé en Ligue des Champions avec Brest, que Léo performe cette saison en Europa League avec Anderlecht, que Dylan s’éclate en MLS... Je suis content pour eux. Quel est ton meilleur souvenir à Sainté ? Sur le plan collectif, les années Batlles, incontestablement ! On va dire la montée en N2. Une montée, c'est toujours agréable. Sur le plan personnel, c'est ma signature professionnelle, et puis l'été 2018 quand j’ai joué avec les pros les matches de préparation.Quel est le plus mauvais souvenir de tes vertes années ? C'est quand je suis parti, je pense ! Mais c’est surtout la tragique disparition de mon coéquipier William Gomis [victime d'une fusillade à La Seyne-sur-Mer il y a 8 ans, ndp2]. Il n’avait même pas 20 ans. Perdre la vie aussi jeune, dans de telles circonstances… Je n’ai pas les mots. On a tous été choqués, bouleversés. Peu de temps avant de décéder, il avait vécu un petit moment chez moi. Je l’avais hébergé, le temps qu’il trouve un appart. William, on ne t’oublie pas !

Le retour du Derby à GG après 3 ans d'absence. Encore un match d'anthologie !

How to drive social impact and business value together

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Companies are often asked to drive business value while simultaneously supporting their communities. In my experience, both goals can be accomplished at the same time. We see it happening in multiple industries.

HP, for example, has connected its social impact efforts directly to its mission of helping people succeed through technology. Through its Future of Work Accelerator, the company supports nonprofits that expand access to digital tools, workforce training, and economic opportunity. The initiative helps more people develop the skills needed in today’s economy while strengthening the broader ecosystem of future workers and entrepreneurs, as well as technology users.

FedEx has taken a similar approach through its Entrepreneur Fund and Boost Camp. Small businesses make up a significant portion of the company’s customer base, and the program provides entrepreneurs with funding, mentorship, and business training to help them grow and scale their ventures. In doing so, FedEx supports local economic growth and job creation, along with small business success. At the same time, FedEx helps bolster the network of businesses that rely on its shipping and logistics services.

These efforts share a common thread: They create programs that benefit individuals and communities in need while advancing company goals.

OUR APPROACH

As NBC celebrates its 100th anniversary this year, we also have an opportunity to make a difference. We challenged ourselves to come up with an innovative way to honor our legacy while investing in the future of the creative industry.

As a media and entertainment company, our social impact efforts are naturally focused on helping the next generation of storytellers build skills, gain meaningful experience, and develop their craft.

That legacy shaped the Power of Storytelling Grants program, developed in partnership with The Alliance for Media Arts + Culture. We selected 10 emerging producers to receive $5,000 grants to create an original 30-second promotional spot honoring NBC’s centennial milestone. Each project drew from the broadcast network’s rich archive, using our iconic intellectual property to recognize our innovation, cultural moments, and industry firsts while fostering the growth of emerging creatives. One winner’s promo was selected to run across Comcast NBCUniversal platforms as part of the company’s anniversary celebration, which culminates in an on-air NBC special on December 10th.

Importantly, the grant recipients also received editorial guidance and mentorship from employee volunteers throughout the process. Access to advisors, professional networks, and real-world assignments can be the difference between aspiring to a dream job and building the key skills that will put a young creator on the path to attaining it.

The participants selected for this program were sourced from nonprofit media arts organizations, including Kids in the Spotlight, New Orleans Video Access Center, PhillyCAM, Reel Works, RE:IMAGINE, and Youth FX. These organizations are helping cultivate creative talent across the country through paid apprenticeships, and our grant program provided them with a rare opportunity for a masterclass in editing with archival footage.

INVEST IN THE NEXT GENERATION

For the winning filmmaker, Danya Granado, the experience represented more than a typical creative assignment. It was a rare moment to engage directly with a century of iconic content and cultural history. As she puts it, “The NBCU archive represents a hundred years of how people have seen news, entertainment, and each other. Getting direct access to that material feels like an invitation to be in conversation with that legacy.”

Stories like Danya’s underscore the value of capitalizing on a company’s greatest assets to invest in the next generation of talent.

Across industries, companies are creating programs that broaden access and strengthen communities. They address real societal needs while also advancing business goals. Whether it’s storytelling, technology, sports, innovation, or another core capability, every business has a superpower. The key is to use it in ways that open doors and create pathways. It should solve real challenges while also advancing strategic objectives.

Yes, it’s community impact. It’s also smart business. When done well, it’s exactly what corporate social responsibility should be: a win-win for the community and the company.

Hilary Smith is executive vice president of corporate social responsibility at NBCUniversal.

PepsiCo layoffs: 1,000 jobs reportedly slashed as CEO pushes soda giant to ‘be faster’ and ‘more capable’

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

November has already been a tough month for layoffs.

In the tech sector, numerous big-name companies, including Amazon, Block, and Squarespace, have already cut roles or are planning to. But workplace reductions are not limited to just tech.

Yesterday, soft drink giant PepsiCo, Inc. (Nasdaq: PEP) reportedly announced that it will lay off around 1,000 employees in North America as sales in the region underperform. Here’s what you need to know.

Pepsi tells employees to prepare for job cuts

PepsiCo CEO Ram Krishnan wrote to employees stating that the company will cut around 1,000 positions in North America in the coming days, multiple media outlets reported.

In the email Krishnan sent to employees (per CNBC), the Pepsi boss said the company’s ambitions were to “build a stronger, more future-fit business that delivers industry-leading growth” in North America.

He added: “To do that, we need to be faster, more connected, and more capable, while continuing to invest in growth — behind our people, capabilities and brands.”

Unfortunately, the endeavor also involves layoffs.

“We will simplify how we operate, better connect teams and strengthen the capabilities we need,” Krishnan wrote. “The goal is to move faster, execute better and invest behind the opportunities that will drive our growth.” The Wall Street Journal first reported the layoffs.

The layoffs will be limited to Pepsi’s North American workforce, which includes roughly 125,000 positions in the United States.

Worldwide, Pepsi employs about 306,000 people as of the end of last year. The layoffs will reportedly happen over the coming week.

Fast Company reached out to PepsiCo for comment.

Pepsi’s North American sales lag other global markets

Last month, Pepsi announced its Q3 2026 financial results, which were broadly respectable. Net revenue increased 5.6%, and operating profit increased 19%.

As CNBC reported at the time, the company’s $25.27 billion in revenue for the quarter exceeded street expectations of $24.96 billion, and its adjusted EPS of $2.34 exceeded the $2.29 analysts had expected.

But Pepsi’s relatively positive results largely came from its international markets, which, year-to-date, have accounted for 41% of its net revenue.

In Pepsi’s home territory of North America, things are less rosy.

The North American food division of Pepsi, which includes products like Frito-Lay snacks, saw volume remain flat, while its beverage business volume declined by 2%.

The lackluster results in North America led Pepsi to reduce its core earnings per share expectations for the full 2026 fiscal year. Previously, Pepsi had expected growth of 5% to 7%. Now it expects growth of 2.5% to 3.5%.

When it came to Pepsi’s North American snacks results, the company blamed the lackluster demand on “the consumer environment.”

Pepsi stock loses its fizz

Pepsi’s North American numbers are the only thing going flat. The company’s share price has lost its fizz over the course of 2026. 

PEP stock began the year performing strongly: By late February, shares were up over 20% since the year began. But since a high above $170 reached in early 2026, Pepsi shares have declined significantly. 

As of yesterday’s close, PEP stock traded at $141. While its share price has increased more than 12% over the past 30 days, that recovery has not been enough to erase the mid-year losses.

As of yesterday’s close, PEP shares were down 1.1% for the year. The broader S&P 500 is up more than 10% over that same period.

But if Pepsi executives hoped layoffs could quickly boost the company’s stock price, they’ll be disappointed. While investors tend to cheer layoffs, since cutting jobs is often the easiest way to quickly improve the bottom line, they seem to have shrugged off the news.

Since the job cuts became public yesterday, PEP shares have hardly moved. Yesterday, Pepsi stock closed up just under four-tenths of a percent, and today in premarket trading, shares are up about the same amount as of the time of this writing.

Paige Paul’s world of Dairy Boy is collabing with Hanes and coming to Target

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Before the lifestyle brand Dairy Boy had a logo or a single follower, Paige Paul—formerly Paige Lorenze—was a New School student crafting a mood board made up of vlogs and pictures.

In 2021, that vision became the Dairy Boy brand—a name inspired by Paul’s love of dairy products like whole milk, which, in the age of oat milk lattes, seemed radical.

Paul, who now has an audience of more than 2.3 million across platforms, filled the brand with products that showcase pieces of her life. Her upbringing on the East Coast and the changing seasons translate into white eyelet cotton dresses in the summer and colorful fleece jackets in the winter. Then there’s the Instagram-ready pajama sets, and a sporty drop just in time for a US Open run by her husband, the American tennis player Tommy Paul. (He made it as far as the Round of 16 this year.)

“Before I had a brand, I feel like I was building a world,” Paige Paul tells Fast Company.

Now, that world of patterns and Americana lore is growing past the sold-out drops and pop-ups with hour-long lines. Dairy Boy is partnering on a limited-edition collaboration with Hanes that will sell online and at more than 1,200 Target stores nationwide beginning November 14.

Dairy Boy’s first major collaboration since it launched five years ago pairs Paul’s Americana dream with a staple of that fantasy: a brand built on comfort and functionality just like hers, but rooted in basics that were lacking in her existing collections.

“Everyone in my life, male or woman, has owned something from Hanes, and I think that is what true heritage legacy brand means in America,” Paul says. “Dairy Boy’s collections are so often vibrant and specific, and I felt like the basic side of our business is something that deserved doing it with the best in class.”

Working alongside Hanes and Target allowed Paul to keep pricing affordable. That’s a priority she traced to her days at the New School, the lower Manhattan parent of the Parsons School of Design and other colleges, when she was looking for a price point she could afford while in college.

The collection keeps the cuts, fabrics, and silhouettes of original Hanes styles, but reimages them through the lens of Dairy Boy. For instance, the collection pairs a classic white tank with men’s boxers, the first time Hanes has sold them specifically for women.

“In my first meetings with Hanes I spoke about how I always stole my husband’s boxers and wore them out to coffee or to bed, and I begged for them to allow me to use their men’s boxers, and they did,” Paul says.

The collection features 45 products, including fluffy bathrobes, waffle pants, long-sleeve sets, a triangle bralette and boy short two-packs, cami sleep dresses, and the husband-inspired boxers. On theme with the drop, small keychains in the shape of a cow wearing boxers will also be available.

While the silhouettes are classic Hanes, the patterns feature some of Dairy Boy’s most recognizable motifs, including lily of the valley, a brown bear print, camo, plaid, blue and brown pinstripe, and tartan.

The collaboration has been in the works for almost two years, as a silent project that grew alongside the brand’s own successes. Dairy Boy tripled its quarterly orders year over year, surpassing total orders from 2025 in the first nine months of 2026. And those who order seem to come back. In the third quarter of 2026, Dairy Boy reported a year-over-year increase of 312% in repeat costumer volume.

The company has grown, Paul says, thanks to its operations that resemble that of a tech company—integrating technology like AI into their back-end systems and keeping the company team lean. The startup-like structure is paired with Paul’s creative direction, which she says is driven by her gut instinct.

“I am really driving the car here, and if there is no data supporting something, but I really believe in a style, we will order it,” Paul says. “There’s no outside investment, so it’s really easy for us to make decisions quickly.”

Despite being the smaller name in the collaboration, Paul sees the Dairy Boy collection as testament to synergy, pairing the brand’s cultural pull with the infrastructure of legacy companies.

“Target has thousands of stores across America. That is something I don’t have,” she says. “The amount of people that I can get to my website for a drop is something that Target doesn’t have.”

Adobe is fully in its agent era

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

I am a big fan of artificial intelligence taking on the miserable tasks of creative work. Renaming and tagging footage, syncing multiple camera angles, reformatting an ad for 50 different aspect ratios? Let the computer do that, thanks. Adobe showed me how its AI agent can do all that, right inside its apps, back in June. But actual creative work? Pass.

Today, at its Max conference, Adobe is announcing something far more radical than anything it showed me just a few months ago. The company is previewing what it calls local MCP integrations with Creative Cloud tools. MCP is the Model Context Protocol, an open standard Anthropic created in late 2024 that acts as a universal adapter, letting any AI agent plug into any software tool.

The integrations would let third-party agents like ChatGPT, Claude, or Gemini work directly inside your local Photoshop, Illustrator, Premiere, and After Effects projects. On your machine. With access to your layers, vector paths, footage bins, and sequences. The projects stay editable by hand, and users will be able to, in Adobe’s words, “step in at any moment.” The apps themselves need to be installed and licensed on your computer, Adobe tells me, because the agent works locally in your project files with the full depth of the desktop tools.

Adobe’s tools already work with third-party AIs—just the other way around. Since December 2025, ChatGPT users have been able to call on Photoshop, Express, and Acrobat to do design work right in OpenAI’s service. And back in April 2026, Adobe launched a connector for Claude to allow it to use more than 50 tools from Photoshop, Illustrator, or Premiere within the Anthropic AI’s cloud-based service. In September, the same ability came to Google.

In all those cases, you drop a file—a PDF, an image, whatever—into a conversation with the AI, ask it to do things to it, and Adobe’s tools will take care of it. The integration allowed users to continue work later in Adobe’s apps.

The new local MCP integrations flip that around, changing who is in control.

In theory, with Adobe’s own AI agent—now rebranded as Adobe Creative Agent—you know that there’s some degree of control and accountability. Until now, Adobe decided how much its agent could do on its own. With MCP, an outside agent gets the same tools, and Adobe can’t vouch for what it does with them. I don’t know about you, but I feel vertigo. Is this ready, even as a beta? Can we trust these agents with our files? And do we really want to?

It seems that plenty of creators are willing to go further in their use of AI. In the Creators’ Toolkit Report that Adobe released alongside these announcements, a survey of more than 2,000 U.S. creators, 77% say the final creative decision should always remain with the human, even when using agents. They want the help. They want to drive the wheel, too.

But there’s an obvious contradiction between the want and what they get. Some 68% of those using creative AI say they at least sometimes settle for results that don’t fully match their vision. Why? Maybe the answer is that 59% say agents save them time. Or that 36% say agents helped them finish projects that were previously too complex. 

Three altitudes of AI

It’s also happening faster than I could have imagined. Forest Key, Adobe’s VP of Firefly and Agentic AI, told me back in June about what he described as three altitudes of AI. The first is the assistant that strips away drudgery. In an example from that launch we saw an agent in Premiere doing its work: taking a pile of randomly named files, sorting them into bins, renaming and tagging clips using computer vision, syncing footage from three cameras with a separate audio track, and dropping a marker on the timeline wherever an interviewer asked a question.

The second altitude is an AI thought partner, while you keep adding your taste and editorial voice. The third is agents that run full automations while you only check in periodically.

Today, Adobe is also previewing always-on agents, coming first to the Firefly AI Assistant, that take on recurring work in the background—organizing incoming assets, checking quality, preparing content for different channels—while creators set the goal, review the plan, and approve the results. That’s a first step toward altitude three. MCP means agents Adobe didn’t build can climb the same ladder. The local MCP integrations won’t enter beta until early 2027.

Agent as partner

Adobe started its agentic push back in April, when it deployed the Adobe Creative Agent in Firefly, in beta mode. This agent could do the work for you, not just vomit generative AI bitmaps. In June, other Adobe programs also got agentic AI capabilities in beta testing: Premiere, Photoshop, Illustrator, InDesign, and Frame.io. That’s when Adobe’s agent started working on your actual projects from inside its apps. After Effects joined the agentic party in September. 

At Adobe Max, the company is adding new capabilities to the Adobe Creative Agent. In Firefly, the assistant can now remember formats, colors, brand guidelines, and characters to apply across projects. A new Plan Mode lets you review, edit, and approve the agent’s plan before it starts, and a model selector lets you pick which AI model does the work. 

The agent is also coming to Premiere mobile in private beta, where it can turn footage into editable first cuts and make edits with plain-language instructions. In desktop Premiere, it can do really complex things, like apply effects across every clip in a sequence, track masked subjects through a frame, and adjust color and exposure across an entire sequence at once.

Until now, there was no official way for outside AI agents to work inside your local projects like Adobe’s agent can. Whenever it becomes available next year, MCP access to Adobe’s apps for agents like Claude or ChatGPT will truly change things. You won’t bring your work to the third-party agent’s home anymore. The third-party agent will come into your home, get its own bedroom, and half of your fridge.

Lingering questions

Having your own ghost artist is a very tempting yet dangerous proposition. What happens if a third-party agent corrupts a sequence, generates something licensing-toxic inside your project, or quietly burns through generative credits? The accountability chain gets blurry fast. 

Anyone could ask Claude to pull reference imagery from the web, then composite it into your project using Photoshop’s own tools to make a document to open and refine yourself. I doubt Adobe’s own assistant will ever do that. First, many of Adobe’s own customers would hate it. And then, imagine the copyright hell that could ensue.

Asked where liability sits between Adobe, the agent provider, and the creator in that scenario, Adobe only pointed back to its answer that MCP is still a preview. When I asked whether third-party agent actions will carry Content Credentials—the provenance standard the company champions—Adobe also told me that it is “working through how provenance will be supported.” As for credit usage, creators will be able to set parameters and review plans before always-on agents run, but on cost Adobe says it is “exploring ways to provide an upfront estimate.”

Those cost questions aren’t only about outside agents, either. Logically, Adobe’s own assistants already burn credits: The After Effects beta started charging them for its AI Assistant on November 6, 2026.

It just feels to me that, for all the good stuff we get from AI helping with work, there’s still a divide when it comes to actual creative work, one that is full of contradictions at many levels. For now, the open questions seem to outnumber the guardrails.

The strategy question

Then there is the matter about what Adobe gains from opening its walled garden to the AI startup hordes. Why give up the last workflow chokepoint it fully owned? 

Maybe they don’t have any options here. Perhaps this is all about the AI agent craze and MCP becoming the standard. Fighting this wave may be seen as worse—especially by greedy stockholders—than riding it. 

What is clear is that the signs of a company-wide pivot to agents are everywhere. A single Adobe Creative Agent now powers the Firefly assistant, the Creative Cloud assistants, and a new private beta in Premiere mobile, while another service called CX Enterprise Coworker in GenStudio orchestrates agents across brands’ content supply chains, according to the company.

“Many creative professionals already use third-party agents that connect to MCP-enabled apps for parts of their production workflow, and they’ve told us they want those agents to work with the tools they trust to finish the job,” Adobe told me in an email. Translation: The customers were already halfway out the door. Let’s keep them in before they find an alternative, integrate it into their workflow, and start canceling subscriptions.

If MCP works as billed, the change will be profound. Your creative software would become a workshop where you hire whichever AI subcontractor you trust—while Adobe repositions itself not as the brain, but as the bench where everyone works. That is either a masterclass in platform strategy or the beginning of Creative Cloud as someone else’s commodity front-end.

Whatever their business strategy is, this new world feels built on shaky grounds. We are climbing the ladder that Key, Adobe’s agentic AI VP, drew for me in June a lot faster than we can build safety checkpoints and best practices rules. As for me, I’m still happy to let a robot rename my footage. But hand Claude the keys to my Premiere timeline? Not yet.

Anthropic stock listing: Target price range hints at valuation lower than rumored reports as IPO date nears

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Rumors have spread for months that Anthropic’s IPO valuation could reach the head-spinning sum of $2 trillion. 

In reality, the creator of Claude is looking at a valuation range of $1 trillion to $1.4 trillion, according to a range of analyses. 

On Tuesday, Anthropic’s updated Securities and Exchange Commission (SEC) filing provided the clearest look yet at what its IPO will look like. 

The AI company plans to offer 825 million shares of Class A common stock at an estimated price of $70 to $78 each. Anthropic is selling more than 801 million shares, while its selling stockholders are offering another 23.6 million shares.

Anthropic is also offering up Class N common stock in a private placement to Google, Nvidia, Advanced Micro Devices (AMD), Broadcom, Micron Technology, Microsoft, and TSMC Partners.

Google and Nvidia will purchase the largest amount at 135,135,135 shares each, worth about $10 million. 

Anthropic predicts the private placement deal will be worth about $26 billion. In total, Anthropic could raise up to $64.35 billion through the IPO. 

The exact date of the Anthropic IPO is still up in the air but likely to be mid-November. The company has applied for listing on Nasdaq under the ticker “ANT.” 

How is Anthropic doing? 

It’s no secret that investors are continuing to fear an AI bubble—with the midterm election results offering more uncertainty about the future of data center buildouts.

Anthropic has seen its growth slow this year. In October, the company reported a 45% increase in revenue for quarter three, down from 60% between quarters one and two.

Following these results, investors had started leaning toward an IPO valuation closer to $1.5 trillion, rather than $2 trillion.

Of course, an IPO valuation over $1 trillion is still remarkable. If accurate, Anthropic will have one of the biggest IPOs of all time—though falling short of SpaceX’s record $1.77 trillion listing earlier this year.

Big Walk’s AI Usage Was Tiny, But Its Disclosure Has Sparked A Bigger Debate

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Big Walk developer House House has confirmed that it used AI during the "final stretch" of its development. The studio recently updated its FAQs page to confirm its use of the tech, with the news coming after 19 Independent Games Festival jurors announced they'll decline to vote for "any game that uses generative AI in any capacity" during the 2027 awards show.

House House hasn't confirmed if its admittance was connected to the IGF--the IGF hasn't named any games either--and the studio said to Aftermath that it estimates "that well under 0.1% of Big Walk’s code" was built with AI. "We have some concern that voluntary disclosures like this might contribute to the normalization of this technology, or its treatment as 'inevitable.' We support the work of those who are trying to limit the harm generative AI is doing to videogames and our world, and apologize that Big Walk’s LLM usage may not meet their standards," House House said. The developer also mentioned that AI was used as a last resort for solving technical issues, as even an engineer at Unity--the engine that Big Walk was developed in--believed that one of their problems wasn't possible to solve.

There's a lot to unpack here, starting with why House House didn't disclose the use of AI for Big Walk. The game is hosted on platforms like Steam, where studios can voluntarily disclose AI usage when it comes to generated code (however, it is mandatory to disclose if a game has AI-generated assets), but House House says it only did so after the topic became a matter of public interest. It also added that it will continue to support developers looking to limit "the harm generative AI is doing to video games and our world."

Pathologic 2 localization writer Bruno Diaz also touched on the controversy, explaining that IGF rules required studios to disclose the use of AI in development. Game designer Austin Walker posted online that the IGF committee has been debating the use of AI in game development, leading 19 jurors to issue a statement. While the group says it doesn't speak for the entire IGF organization or all jurors involved in the IGF Awards, IGF leadership supports the statement. Overall, if AI is used in any part of a game's development, from coding to visual assets, the jurors will not vote for it.

"Programming is a creative discipline whose labor deserves the same respect we extend to any part of making a game," the IGF juror's statement reads. "This extends to the past work used to train these models without the creators' consent."

The IGF's stance comes as the discussion over AI heats up in the games industry. Proponents argue it'll make video game development faster and more affordable; others argue it contributes to environmental harm and is leading to job losses across multiple fields. Others have argued that House House's use of AI to solve challenging coding bugs isn't a big deal, but critics say the AI disclosure should have been there from the beginning.

At the same time, the frequency of AI slop games has exploded in recent months, with the October edition of Steam Next Fest being a dumping ground for hastily vibe-coded game demos. Copycats have also become a major headache, with several game developers withdrawing their demos from the public over fears that bad actors will copy their work and rush a clone to market.

Announcing Long-Context Training on TPUs with MaxText Context Parallelism

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Long-context training — including pretraining and post-training, has become a foundation of frontier AI models. Longer native sequence lengths give large language models (LLMs) the working memory to read a whole code repository or ingest hours of video, and reason through multi-step agentic tasks. In three years, context windows have grown from 128K–200K tokens to 1M–2M, and models trained toward 10M tokens are arriving. Training at those lengths, not just serving them, is where the system limits show up.

In particular, efficient training at multi-million-token sequence lengths runs into the following bottlenecks:

Today, we are excited to announce long-context training support in MaxText, Google Cloud's open-source JAX training framework. MaxText enables this by supporting four context-parallel strategies on Cloud TPU: All-gather, A2A (DeepSpeed Ulysses), P2P (Ring Attention), and the A2A + P2P hybrid (Unified Sequence Parallelism, or USP), combined with load balancing and sequence packing. All four use the Tokamax Splash Attention kernel. Combined with the 3D inter-chip interconnect (ICI) ( supported on TPUs such as TPU 5p or TPU 7 (Ironwood) ), this lets one sequence span an entire slice while communication overlaps compute and memory per chip stays flat. In our benchmarks, Llama 3.1 8B (25 head dim, 16 query heads) demonstrates exceptional long-context efficiency, achieving 70% Model FLOPs Utilization (MFU) on TPU 5p for sequence lengths up to 4 million tokens. Across both TPU 5p and TPU 7, context can be seamlessly extended to 14 million tokens—with TPU 7 consistently maintaining 50% MFU across all sequence scales below 6 million tokens.

This functionality is available today in MaxText v0.2.4(doc).

Context parallelism splits a long sequence across chips, so no chip holds the activations of the whole sequence. Context parallelism can be combined with data and tensor parallelism, and the smallest context-parallel size that fits your sequence in HBM is usually the right one. This also holds for sparser attention variants which cut attention compute but not the activations of every other layer.

Attention is the one global operation where every token needs the keys and values of every preceding token, so each context parallel strategy has to bring those keys and values back together. Each makes a different tradeoff between memory (how much KV a chip holds), communication (how much moves per layer, and whether it overlaps compute), and model shape (how many attention heads to split).

All-gather: Each chip keeps its chunk of the sequence and, at every layer, gathers the keys and values from every other chip. It is the simplest option. However, because causal masking causes later tokens to attend to larger prefixes, All-gather requires balancing compute load across devices.

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

All-to-All (Ulysses): Gathering keys and values makes every chip hold the whole sequence, so attention memory never shrinks no matter how many chips you add. All-to-All changes what each chip owns instead. Attention is independent across heads but not across tokens, so the head axis is free to shard and the token axis is not. An All-to-All before attention rotates the sharding from one onto the other: each chip gives up the heads it is not responsible for and receives the tokens it was missing for the heads it keeps, ending up with the full sequence for a few heads instead of a few tokens for all heads. This is also why Ulysses does not need token load balancing. Attention then runs as ordinary single-chip attention with standard kernels, and a second All-to-All rotates the output back. KV per chip shrinks by the context-parallel factor, but you cannot split across more chips than the model has heads — and tensor parallelism shards that same dimension, so TP × CP is capped by the head count (under GQA, by the KV heads: eight for Llama 3 8B). The all-to-all itself, though, does not overlap with compute — attention cannot start on a partial sequence, so every chip waits for data from every other chip before any of them begins.

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

P2P (ring attention): Ulysses' ceiling is the head count; ring attention removes it by never leaving the token axis. Keys and values never materialize in full. KV chunks travel around a ring of chips one hop at a time, neighbor to neighbor. Each chip attends its queries to the chunk it holds while the next one is in flight, and merges the partial results as it goes. A chip holds its own chunk and one visiting chunk, never the full sequence, and the communication overlaps compute. However, dividing attention computation into smaller chunks can reduce local kernel efficiency, and unlike All-to-All Ulysses, ring attention requires explicit load balancing.

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

A2A + P2P (USP): A2A + P2P (USP): Neither strategy scales on its own — All-to-All stops at the head count, and a long ring accumulates hops while thinning the overlap that makes it work. Unified sequence parallelism composes them on two axes of the chip mesh, so the context-parallel degree becomes the product of the two: All-to-All along one axis, sized at or below the KV-head count, and a ring along the other to extend past it. Because each All-to-All group has already pooled its tokens, a ring step now carries a chunk larger by the All-to-All factor, and the ring is correspondingly shorter — more compute per hop to hide the transfer behind, and fewer hops to accumulate latency and causal imbalance. On Cloud TPU the two map onto two axes of the slice's chip mesh.

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

These strategies cover the attention variants in today's major models, from multi-head and grouped-query attention to DeepSeek-style multi-head latent attention.

Splitting the sequence only pays off if the attention compute on each chip stays efficient. All four strategies run on Splash Attention, the block-sparse flash attention kernel from Tokamax, Google's open-source library of TPU kernels. It skips fully masked blocks and handles packed sequences without padding. P2P calls it once per ring step, on the local queries and the KV chunk that has just arrived, while the transfer for the next step runs underneath, in the forward and backward pass alike. Each chip does the same fused, block-level work it would do without context parallelism, with the communication hidden behind it.

P2P (Ring Attention) is the primary workhorse for very long-context training, whereas All-Gather is the standard baseline for most other cases where the KV can fit. These are the primary CP strategies to use in practice. We compare how far each can push context length on a fixed 64-chip TPU 5p slice with Llama 3.1 8B (256 head dim, 16 query heads) , and measure their MFUs. In ideal settings where both strategies can have large enough chunks (S/CP) and local batch size - the two major variables driving MFU on TPU 5p, we observe that P2P can outperform All-Gather almost every context length from 64K to 14M tokens, where communication becomes mostly hidden with the Ring circular transport schedule.

TPU v7x (Ironwood) takes the same recipe further. With twice the HBM per chip, P2P training of 14M tokens on 64 TPU 7 chips reached 48.7% Model FLOPs Utilization (MFU) compared to 37.1% on TPU 5p. We observed that ring attention performs better than other context parallelism strategies -- e.g. Ulysses and USP –- on TPU 7 chips.

The choice of context parallelism strategy depends mostly on whether the gathered KV fits in HBM, and on how many KV heads the model has:

For all CP strategies, when you run low on memory: Offload before you recompute. MaxText can move saved activations, optimizer states, and parameters to host memory tensor by tensor. At long context lengths, these transfers are hidden behind compute, making offloading effectively free. Offload attention outputs first, followed by optimizer states, layer inputs, parameters, and finally remat as a last resort. MFU stays flat through the offload steps while all-gather falls off and eventually stops fitting. For more details, see the remat tuning guide.

Looking ahead, we see two natural extensions. The first is broader model coverage. Models that mix sliding-window and global layers, such as Gemma, would run the window layers with a short exchange between neighboring chips while the global layers use the ring. We also plan to support linear attention mechanisms such as Gated Delta Net (GDN) and Kimi Delta Attention (KDA). The second is performance, with further tuning of the Tokamax kernels and the P2P backward pass to narrow the remaining gap to all-gather at short context, and recipes by model and context length for TPU 5p and 7 chips.

Context parallelism is available today in MaxText v0.2.4 on PyPI, the corresponding Docker images and on GitHub main. Sample recipes are available here for TPU 7 and TPU 5p. Pick a strategy with context_parallel_strategy (ring, ulysses, or usp), set the context-parallel size, and keep the rest of your run the same. The sharding guide has the flags and constraints for each strategy, and the release notes link to the pull requests behind this work. We can't wait to see what you train.

While I agree that some people are being prescribed GLP-1s that probably should not be (in the sense that they could achieve similar efficacy through non-pharmaceutical interventions such as diet/exercise), I think for a significant majority of recipients, the metabolic effects of this class of drugs is profound and their benefit far outweighs the risk. Of course, we see clinically significant reductions not only in HbA1c and stronger blood glucose control for T2DM patients as well as prediabetics--as these are the originally intended drug targets--but we also see concomitant reductions in cardiovascular risk, significant reductions in obesity, improved activities of daily living, and an interesting inhibitory effect on non-appetite related cravings (e.g., alcohol, tobacco, and drug use) that was not originally foreseen.

To say that these drugs are too risky and overprescribed based on rare but serious side effects is to dismiss huge benefits for a population of patients whose comorbidities were uncontrolled and essentially had one foot in the grave.

Again, I want to emphasize that GLP-1s are not for everyone, not even for everyone with obesity or T2DM. They have profound effects on the metabolism. We knew this ever since exenatide was developed over a decade ago. That's why they are prescription only. You need to have a doctor evaluate your use on a regular basis. People need to understand that we are all unique in our response to drugs, and that the "average" person doesn't exist. That's why we do clinical trials and postmarketing surveillance--which is also how we caught this particular risk.

Nobody (except anti-science assholes with an agenda) argues against allowing chemotherapy for late stage cancer patients even though they are cytotoxic. You weigh the risk against the benefit. If chemotherapy means one might get 6 months more life, even if it's not pain-free, that's up to the patient to decide. To paint it with a broad brush and argue for denying anyone such treatment because a small fraction of patients won't respond or will experience serious side effects, is barbaric and insane. Yet people hear about some rare but serious side effect in a drug with literal life-saving potential, and demand it be withdrawn from market. That's equally insane.

Yes, there are people who are getting access to this class of drugs who probably don't need it. This phenomenon is not unique to GLP-1s--it's just particularly noticeable in this case because of our society's emphasis on physical appearance. But to suggest that the incidence of obesity was lower in previous generations as some justification for restricting access is wild. It offers no solution for the social, cultural, and economic reasons for the rise in obesity in the first place, so it rings hollow to say GLP-1s are overprescribed while staying silent about the predatory practices of the food industry and how they manipulate the consumption habits of people; the dismantling of physical and dietary education programs in schools; the lack of funding and political resistance to providing healthy school meals; the existence of "food deserts" and proliferation of fast food in economically depressed areas; etc. Medical professionals have been watching this trend with horror and have been repeatedly telling everyone to diet and exercise for the past 50+ years, to no avail. It's not like they haven't tried. The insinuation that the vast majority of obese people are obese by choice and/or lack of sufficient self-control, is an old, old trope that serves no one and needs to die. It doesn't make any sense in a society that is so fixated on superficial physical appearance that people are actively shunned for being overweight.

You should try Gleam

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

If you have read my recent rant about programming languages, you'll know I've been quite interested in Gleam. Gleam is a functional language that targets BEAM, the Erlang VM,1 with a simple syntax and interoperability with the wider BEAM ecosystem (Erlang and Elixir). Interestingly, Gleam can also target JavaScript i.e., Gleam can be compiled into JavaScript and run through Node.js.

I've been mostly using Rust for hobby projects, which at the moment is the only language that really makes coding enjoyable for me, and Gleam has managed to scratch that itch too. If you're a Rust connaisseur, then Gleam will feel familiar to you, bar a few key differences. I feel like 80% of the code I write could be copy pasted to Rust (with a tinge of search-and-replace for syntax) and it'd compile.

This post won't give an exhaustive overview of the language, if you want to learn more, I recommend the excellent interactive tour. You'll get a good feel for Gleam in under half an hour.

On its homepage, Gleam is described as "a friendly language for building type-safe systems that scale!" (emphasis theirs) which does a good job of conveying what Gleam is all about.

The syntax is extremely simple, most of the complex stuff is hidden by the Erlang runtime which you don't really have to interact with unless you want to! Of course, Gleam is a functional language, so if you have lived in a C-shaped imperative world your whole life, it might be a bit daunting at first. But if you compare it to other functional languages (OCaml, Haskell, Erlang), you'll quickly notice Gleam is much simpler. After using it a bit, it feels like all the functional parts of Rust, with a few missing ones, and none of the imperative parts.

Let's start from an example piece of code:

Dissecting this a bit, you can see a few specificities of Gleam:

Comparing with the Rust code snippet, you can see that, up to a few naming choices, they look pretty similar. The main difference in this example is really the piping vs. chaining syntax. Gleam types have no methods so piping is used instead.

The piping operator |> is the first of the three mascot features of Gleam you will meet in this post, the other two being the use pattern and tail-call optimization.

If you've written a lot of JavaScript, you might be used to the indentation creep that arises when nesting many callbacks into one another

In Gleam, many functions expect a callback, for example because as a way to control access to a resource and clean up when the process is done (closing a database connection, releasing a file handle, etc.). To avoid this kind of tower of callbacks, Gleam provides some syntactic sugar: use param <- some_func(arg) is equivalent to some_func(arg, fn(param) { ... }). The callback must always be the last argument of the function. That is the opposite of |>, which fills the first one.

This one might genuinely trip you up if you're not familiar with it. The first thing you need to know is there are no loops in Gleam! Again, if you've written OCaml or Haskell before, you won't be surprised by this. In 98% of cases,4 loops are simple operations over bounded iterations. Think for loops on a list or a set. Exactly the kind of thing which in Rust you always have two ways of doing: one imperative with the exact for loop, one more functional with a combination of operations on iterators. Well, if you look at the code example in the previous section, you'll see Gleam uses the second kind and has a plethora of functions to iterate over lists, sets, tuples, strings, anything.

But what if you need something more complicated? What about the 2%? This is where you would instead use recursion! A simple algorithm which illustrates this is Euclid's algorithm to compute the Greatest Common Divisor (GCD). Below you can find a Rust iterative example, and a Gleam recursive example.

Of course, you can also write the recursive version in Rust, but this usually isn't such a great idea. Recursion in imperative languages can quickly get out of hand: every call adds a new stack frame, and a deeply recursive function can blow the stack (the infamous stack overflow).

On the BEAM there is no fixed-size stack, so runaway recursion eats heap memory until the process is killed instead, but the point remains: deep recursions equals trouble. To avoid that problem, compilers for functional languages implement a technique called Tail Call Optimization (TCO), which applies when the recursive call is the last thing the function does. The existing stack frame is then reused instead of stacking a new one, giving performance close to an imperative while loop.5

This piece of the slogan is mostly there to answer the obvious question "why Gleam and not Elixir?" (another Erlang-based language). While Elixir is a dynamically-typed language (with gradual typing now!), Gleam was built as a statically-typed language with a strong type system: strong types are its raison d'Ãªtre.

A type in Gleam encompasses both the enum and the struct concepts in Rust:

Because Gleam types are algebraic data types, they are very useful to ensure undesired states are impossible. A great aspect of Gleam, which will be relevant a little bit later, is that it compiles to both the BEAM virtual machine and to JavaScript. So you can share these strong type definitions across different applications in both targets. This is particularly handy if you need a web application to communicate with a BEAM app.

That, of course, refers to BEAM, the Erlang VM. Unlike languages which target the native system (Rust, Go, C++), a VM provides an extra abstraction layer between the system and the program. In the case of BEAM, that layer is exceptionally good at handling concurrent processes and failures while remaining fast.

Gleam makes good use of that, and this is what a concurrent program would look like:

What you should take away from this code is that it's the BEAM that handles processes.

The call to process.spawn_unlinked triggers a new process with no link to its creator, so when order 102 crashes, neither the parent nor siblings are affected. With a plain process.spawn the child would be linked to the caller, and that panic would have taken the whole program down. If you look closely, I also added process.sleep(10) so the parent wouldn't close before the children could finish: this is a hack, since the delay is only a guess and a slower child would still be cut off.

Thankfully, a great advantage of the BEAM is how easy inter-process communication is, which the following code piece illustrates well:

In the paragraph above, I briefly showed how the BEAM virtual machine is great for concurrent programs communicating by message passing. If you're familiar with Go, this is similar to how it handles the same problem with goroutines. But the OTP (Open Telecom Platform), the set of libraries that ships with Erlang, is more powerful than that. It provides you with a built-in supervisor-actor paradigm which enables you to create scalable, concurrent services which can crash without taking down the whole application.

In a nutshell, the OTP exposes actors: lightweight processes that hold state and communicate with each other via messages (like in the example above). When paired with a supervisor, the whole application becomes more resilient to any individual actor crashing: the supervisor will monitor processes and restart them as needed. It can also help start/stop groups of actors dynamically.

If you're reading this and thinking "uh that sounds a lot like how I used Kubernetes", you're not alone. The OTP model is extremely similar to the controller-pod architecture of Kubernetes and solves a lot of the same problems.6 However, in my experience, a lot of workflows using Kubernetes don't really need a cluster but still use one (and that's expensive!) because of the added bonus of the distributive experience. For those cases, the OTP provides a really interesting alternative.

Let me give you a concrete example. Without going in too much detail, we have a common setup in my team which involves a handwritten Go job scheduler handling some other processes.

The scheduler, each worker, and the user interface are all pods inside the cluster, managed by Kubernetes. To be honest, this works well, but it does introduce a lot of complexity. First of all, most of these services do not need to be so isolated from each other. The consequence here is that we need duplicate REST APIs in various places to enable communication between different services.

What if we wanted to reorganize this within a Gleam application? There are two parts where Gleam would shine here:

Within the BEAM application, all actors would communicate via messages:

So instead of handling the deployment of multiple applications in separate pods, you can just let the OTP handle all process management within one VM (for the scheduling part).

As mentioned before, one of the big advantages of Gleam is that it compiles to JavaScript. So you can bundle shared type definitions between the user interface and the job scheduler instead of keeping duplicate APIs in sync.

Every language has its flaws, and it's time for some honest feedback on Gleam. I don't pretend I have much experience with the language yet, so if any of these complaints feel unfair to you, feel free to correct me. So far, these are the things I noticed:

Of course this is a very biased account. I think I'm missing a lot of flaws in the language itself, flaws that will show themselves once the honeymoon period is over. But for now, I'm having a blast with Gleam, and I can only encourage you to give it a try too! I'm sure just like me, you'll have a lot of fun!

If like me this doesn't mean much to you, Erlang is also a functional language, albeit a much older one (about 40 years old), whose primary use has been for telecom systems. The BEAM shines with distributed, concurrent, fault-tolerant systems (so, telecom, obviously). ↩︎

One important thing about immutable languages is that when you do something like let new_list = list.take([1, 2, 3], 2), you're not making a copy of the data (that'd be very wasteful), instead the common data in both variables comes from the same spot in the memory. ↩︎

Gleam also has the Option monad via gleam/option, but unlike Rust, it's somewhat discouraged and a simple Result(T, Nil) is preferred when possible. This is why reduce in Gleam returns Result(T, Nil) while Rust returns Option. ↩︎

This is a completely made-up statistics, but I feel it's not too far from reality. ↩︎

Rust does not guarantee TCO. LLVM sometimes applies sibling-call optimisation, but it is not something you can rely on, and explicit tail calls are still unstable. Since Rust also gives you loops, the imperative style is generally the safer choice. Compilers for functional languages are usually much more aggressive here, since TCO is crucial to their performance. ↩︎

The main difference is really about isolation: an OTP node is a single OS process, and all the actors on it share the same memory, while Kubernetes gives each pod its own container and spreads them over the machines of the cluster. Erlang does know how to cluster several nodes together, but OTP has no cluster scheduler, no placement and no container isolation: you still have to deploy and place the nodes yourself. ↩︎

Gleam's exact age is debatable. According to Wikipedia, Gleam was initially created in 2016, but it would only be considered a "serious" endeavour starting from 2019 (when it got numbered versioning) and only reached version 1.0.0 in 2024. ↩︎

The hardest tarball

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Personal and work related blogof Harmen Stoppels.

Open source software developerat Stoppels Consultingin Zürich, Switzerland.

Find me on GitHubor send me an email atfirstname[at]lastname.ch

Here is a curiosity: an archive that can be constructed using a single GNU tar invocation, that is extracted in 8 distinct ways depending on what tool you use.

It creates an archive hardest.tar from three files file ./file empty in sequence, where file is repeated so that GNU tar stores the second one as a hard link, and empty is empty. Exploiting the unique path of the second entry, the hard link is renamed to cafÃ©. This triggers tar to create a pax header to store the file name as UTF-8. We add a size=512 record to the pax header, which overrides the default size=0 from the hard link. The value 512 is not a coincidence: it is exactly the size of a tar header.

Visually, this archive has the following structure, with two basic interpretations based on whether a hard link can have a size or not.

Now, POSIX says that data blocks for a hard link “may be included” in pax archives, but the implications of this are not entirely clear. So the 512 bytes after the hard link are its data, and not the header of empty as we archived it? How can a hard link have different contents from its previous counterpart file?

This archive gives by far the richest and most surprising results I've encountered so far when extracting it with well-known tar utilities and libraries. The results below were produced using the latest releases of all packages at the time of writing.

This group is reasonable in principle: they ignore the size on the hard link, and extract file, its hard link cafÃ©, and empty.

This is the system tar utility of macOS, which is closer to POSIX in spirit regarding the concept of size on hard links, and writes the 512 bytes into the hard link. The end result is that file now contains the tar header of empty.

This group agrees with bsdtar that the 512 bytes are data of the hard link, but doesn't write them into the hard link. Instead, they skip forward over the data, and you are left with just file and cafÃ©, without empty.

This is where you begin to need more imagination. Because cafÃ© contains data, a separate file is created that contains the header of empty as data, while file keeps its original contents.

Makes cafÃ© a separate, empty file, and also extracts empty. Why not?

Does not extract cafÃ© at all, but to make up for it, it creates an empty directory PaxHeaders next to file and empty.

R has an entirely different approach: it extracts empty with 512 null bytes. It mistakenly applies the size to empty instead of the hard link, and those 512 bytes are from an end-of-archive marker.

Haskell's tar would be exactly like GNU tar, if only it did not have string decoding issues, which turn the file cafÃ© into cafÃÂ© on disk (in the year 2026, this is not a rendering quirk of your browser).

These implementations reject the tarball. I believe this is the best thing you can do, even if it's technically not POSIX-compliant.

This tarball is one of 134 tricky tarballs that I'm collecting and categorizing in my test suite haampie/tar-minefield, developed since the last post. Its website https://stoppels.ch/tar features a big table that shows how implementations deal with all these cases, as well as detailed info per test case. A variant from the tarball of this post is included as a test case.

If you'd like to discuss this, send me an email at firstname[at]lastname.ch.

Europe is about to get very tough on loot boxes in games, according to a leaked draft of the landmark Digital Fairness Act

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

"The digital era has fundamentally transformed consumer experiences..."

An alleged draft of the potentially game-changing Digital Fairness Act has leaked ahead of its scheduled publication tomorrow (11th November), containing proposals that could dramatically affect the way games operate and are monetised within the European Union.

The draft, published by French publication Contexte (via Video Games Industry Memo) goes as far as to recommend that loot boxes (uncertainty-based rewards) be turned off by default for minors, as well as the sale of in-game items and currencies. There's also a recommendation that all players - not just minors - should be able to turn off offers related to these lootboxes and gambling mechanics.

"The digital era has fundamentally transformed consumer experiences," the draft of the Digital Fairness Act begins. "It has enabled faster, more convenient and wider access to an ever-increasing range of goods and services. At the same time, it has given rise to new and more sophisticated ways of influencing consumers' choices.

"Along with the indisputable benefits in choice and efficiency, the extensive use of digital technologies allows both old and new unfair commercial practices to affect consumers at scale, exacerbating risks to their economic interests, as well as to their mental and physical wellbeing. Vulnerable consumers are particularly affected."

The Digital Fairness Act calls for greater transparency around a "transactional decision". Which means: "Price transparency on the real value of in-app purchases using virtual currencies and the probabilities of winning for virtual offers with uncertainty-based rewards (loot boxes).

"All consumers can turn off virtual offers with uncertainty-based rewards. Some spending-maximising practices prohibited for minors, with the remaining turned off by default. Supporting self-regulation for video games and legal obligation for video games to have an age-appropriate rating. All consumers can turn off attention-maximising features, with some prohibited for minors and the remaining turned off by default."

"Spending-maximising" refers to systems that pressure us to spend more money than we want to, such as reward systems or pay-to-progress or pay-to-win systems. They're all being targeted here, as is virtual currency, which provides a layer of abstraction as to the real cost of goods.

"It may be difficult for minors to resist such perceived pressure to pay," the Digital Fairness Act said with regard to progression-linked systems. "Therefore, where a product is directed at minors or when the trader is aware with reasonable certainty that the product is used by minors, pay-to-progress or pay-to-win mechanisms [...] should not be made available to minors."

The Digital Fairness Act is a forthcoming European legislative proposal designed to update consumer protection rules for various digital dangers that we face today. Importantly, it's not law yet, and it has iterations and discussions to go through before it will be. A timeline on the Digital Fairness Act explainer website suggests it could be adopted late in 2027, then applied more broadly by 2030.

It's also hoping to tackle many things besides shady video game monetisation, including "problematic" influencer marketing, "unfair" pricing practices - such as obscuring the real cost of something until you've progressed through layers trying to buy it - and mysterious-sounding "dark patterns". These relate to 'easy to sign-up, hard-to-cancel' processes, it seems.

These changes aren't happening in a vacuum. Earlier this year, the well-known European age-rating body PEGI announced historic alterations to its game-rating criteria, with an eye to better categorising the dangers of loot box-containing games and aggressively monetised games. These changes have already started to roll out and mean that a game like EA Sports FC, which was previously routinely rated PEGI 3, now automatically gets a PEGI 16 rating because of its Ultimate Team card pack (loot box) system.

The effect of the Digital Fairness Act could tip the balance against these systems being included in games at all. Expect more discussion and digestion of the proposed changes as the Act is officially published tomorrow.

Nintendo Switch, Nintendo Switch 2, PC, PS4, PS5, Xbox One, Xbox Series X/S

Android, Mac, Nintendo Switch, Nintendo Switch 2, PC, PS4, PS5, Xbox One, Xbox Series X/S, iOS

Nintendo Switch, Nintendo Switch 2, PC, PS4, PS5, Xbox One, Xbox Series X/S

Microsoft are rejecting your mail

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

On Friday morning, we became aware that a significant amount of email to Microsoft-run mail systems was being rejected. This included mail to hotmail.com and outlook.com.

The messages were being rejected with the following message:

550 5.7.1 Message rejected: Message lacks SPF/DKIM/DMARC Auth Records

We checked our logs, and found that we’d seen this message over 500 times on Thursday, but not once before that.

The affected messages all had completely valid SPF and DKIM, but did not have a DMARC record. We added a DMARC record with a “none” policy to some of the affected domains, and sure enough, mail was then accepted again. A quick fedi-survey confirmed that we were not alone.

We don’t normally re-use blog photos, but this one seemed too perfect.

DMARC records are a way of publicly stating what you want recipients to do with email that claims to be from your domain, but which doesn’t pass verification checks (DKIM or SPF) and thus is potentially forged. Your DMARC policy can be one of the following:

Publishing a policy of none is effectively the same as not publishing a policy at all.

Microsoft are now rejecting mail without a DMARC policy, but accepting mail with a policy of none. This is a massive and pointless change in their policy for incoming mail, made with no notice whatsoever.

Last year Microsoft, introduced a policy that required domains sending more than 5,000 messages per day to have a DMARC record. This change was announced in advance. This latest change appears to be completely unannounced, which makes us wonder if it was even intentional. Of course, trying to contact Microsoft in order to resolve mail issues such as this is futile.

Update: apparently it wasn’t intentional, but they haven’t figured out how to roll it back yet.

We’ve just applied p=none DMARC records to a large number of our customers’ domains. We generally try to avoid making unsolicited changes to DNS zones, but in this case we feel that it is the best option.

If you’re in contact with Microsoft users affected by this, please encourage them to complain to Microsoft; it’s very likely that they are losing other legitimate email from other providers because of this unannounced change. Better still, encourage them to move to an email provider that is pro-active in investigating email deliverability issues.

Mythic Beasts is a privately owned hosting ISP with a focus on a no-nonsense service backed by excellent technical support

We realise that the range of options can be daunting, and are always very happy to offer advice on what makes sense for you.

Drop us an email and we'll do our best to help.
