Open Graph images with Astro and Typst

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Links to blog articles look better when they’re accompanied by an image. If the HTML head has a meta tag like this, it will happen automatically:

My previous, tedious workflow for these kinds of images was to go to unspash.com, search for something like structure or archive, and then select an image I thought would fit the theme of the post. Being both boring and error-prone, I abandonded that process and set up a pipeline that generates image cards at build-time instead. They look like this:

Typst is a tool that generates high-quality documents from a simple markup language. A lovechild of LaTeX and Markdown, it works great for math whilst also keeping the day-to-day text formatting syntax bearable. As a small taste, this snippet (adapted from the Typst README)

By default the typst CLI will create a PDF, but it can also make PNGs. The above image was created with the command

But if you’d rather create something with a bit more pizzazz, by all means. How about

If all of my posts were titled “Typst very nice”, we’d be done, but, alas. Luckily, typst accepts inputs to a document with --input key=value, which is put in a sys.inputs dictionary for the template to read. So the template becomes

Let’s move on to generating the images at build-time.

This site is built with Astro, a static site generator. Articles are written in Markdown, which is then bundled into a Content Collection by Astro. The details are irrelevant, what matters is that this lets me define metadata which I can query through a Javascript API.

The source code for the page template is at src/pages/posts/[id].astro. There are a few notable things about this file path:

In other words, Astro needs to know every ID when building the site—it can’t generate infinitely many IDs. A function called getStaticPaths exported from the template defines the set:

That is, fetch every article, and use its id as the id parameter. This is the object returned for the current page:

By default Astro will generate an HTML file, but it can also return other kinds of responses. Together with this blog post I also put the following snippet at src/pages/api/build-info.json.ts:

Note that there are no parameters in brackets, so this will create a single JSON file at build time:

And just like that, this blog now has a JSON api.

Note that the file extension is included in the path, i.e. .json.ts. This is because when the dev server is running it sets the appropriate content-type header to let the browser know what to do with the response, but once the page is built the result is a file with .ts stripped off. The browser then interprets the remaining file extension to know what content to expect at the path.

You probably see where this is going: Create an API route which takes a post ID as a parameter and ends in .png.ts, gets the post title using the Astro Content Collection API, generates an image, and returns it in a response with the appropriate content-type header.

You got it. Here’s the open graph image endpoint in its entirety, defined at src/pages/posts/[id]/og.png.ts:

Now go to eaj.no/posts/open-graph-images-with-typst/og.png, et voilà:

When a sharing platform loads the image for an article, it might very well be cached for a long time. Even if you decide to completely redo your entire branding, LinkedIn might insist on displaying your previous hideous design unless you handle it. A simple trick is to use a throwaway search parameter that doesn’t do anything other than make the URL unique:

A straightforward (but silly) solution for creating this parameter is to generate a random string of characters every time the site is built, ensuring your image is always fetched anew. I landed on using a hash of the typst template and the title, so that the cache key only changes whenever the actual content does:

InfoQ Homepage News Effect 4.0 Ships: Dependency-Free Rewrite with 5x Smaller Bundles and Long-Term Support

Nov 10, 2026 3 min read

Effect, the TypeScript framework for building production-grade applications with typed errors, structured concurrency and dependency injection, has released Effect 4.0. Published on 30 September 2026, it is the first stable version of a line that spent eight months in beta and six weeks in release candidate.

Effect 4.0 is a from-scratch rewrite of the fiber runtime, aimed at smaller bundles, lower memory use and higher throughput. The team's own figures put a minimal program using Effect, Stream and Schema at 7.1 kB against 35.6 kB in 3.x, a 5x reduction. The heap used by 50,000 fibers drops from 157.5 MB to 21.8 MB, an 86% cut, and concurrent task throughput moves from 0.71 million to 4.57 million tasks per second, roughly 6.4x. The numbers are medians of nine fresh-process runs on identical source, and as noted by stork.ai, have not been independently reproduced.

Alongside the rewrite, the ecosystem has been consolidated. Core modules from @effect/platform, @effect/rpc and @effect/cluster now live inside the main effect package, every @effect/* package shares a single version, and the core ships with zero runtime dependencies after the final removal of pg (replaced by a native PgProtocol client) and MessagePack.

With 4.0, the team has introduced a long-term support policy. Effect 4.x receives bug fixes until September 2029, or one year after 5.0 ships, whichever is later. Security fixes run until September 2029 or two years after 5.0, again whichever is later.

Stork.ai points out that the 43.9 million weekly downloads cited at launch covers the whole ecosystem across beta and RC tags, while stable Effect 4.0 itself recorded "approximately 150,000 downloads on its first day".

On HackerNews, one commenter has been using the beta for some time and has had a very positive experience:

I’ve been using the beta on three separate projects for quite a while now and it has been totally pain-free. I mostly use the v3x core library (which has expanded to contain other libraries in v4) and Schema so I can’t speak to much outside of those, but I trust they’ve done a great job all around.

I don’t see it mentioned here, but 4 also comes with a much simpler API in some modules. Schema is much better. It’s an exceptional update. I followed many of the video logs on YouTube and was impressed by the team. This is one of my favourite software projects, period. This rebuild is incredibly well done, and I hope a lot of people are willing to try it out and adopt it, especially with the LTS.

The team's own Effect 4.0: The First Week post, published alongside patch releases 4.0.1 and 4.0.2 on 8 October, admitted that several modules intended to ship as unstable went out without a @stability tag, implicitly promising they were stable. The missing tags were added, and every exported API now declares its stability explicitly. The same window moved HTTP, SQL, RPC and AI spans to the OpenTelemetry semantic conventions.

Effect 4.1 followed on 20 October 2026 with new effect/crypto modules covering randomness, hashing and signing, a Glob module backed by FileSystem.glob on Node, Bun and Deno, a streaming Csv module in effect/encoding, and support for RFC 9457 Problem Details in HttpApi.

For teams on 3.x, the v3 to v4 migration guide is the starting point, with the team suggesting coding agents can handle most of the mechanical work. Effect sits in similar territory to ZIO in the Scala world, alongside TypeScript libraries such as Effection 4.0 that target structured concurrency with a deliberately smaller surface.

Effect is an open-source TypeScript framework maintained by Effectful Technologies, providing typed error handling, structured concurrency, dependency injection and observability primitives for both frontend and backend applications.

InfoQ Homepage News Google Cloud Spend Caps Reach General Availability with API and Monthly Resets

Nov 10, 2026 3 min read

Google Cloud has made spend caps generally available on Cloud Billing Budgets, letting customers pause new usage of a service in a project once a monthly budget is reached, without deleting resources. The GA release adds an API, estimated live costs that trigger caps within minutes for AI services, and automatic monthly resets, but enforcement remains limited to Gemini API, the Gemini Enterprise Agent Platform, Cloud Run, and Cloud Run functions.

A spend cap is a budget with enforcement attached: according to the documentation, when estimated spend on the capped service reaches the target amount, new requests to that service in that project are blocked, in-flight requests run to completion and are charged, persistent resources keep running, and nothing is deleted. Usage covered by committed-use discounts is paused along with pay-as-you-go usage, while Provisioned Throughput for AI models is never halted. Billing administrators and project owners receive alerts at 50, 80, and 100 percent of the cap.

The company changed three things between the July public preview and the GA release:

The limits are as specific as the features: a cap covers one project and one service for a calendar month, so folders, organizations, labels and multi-service budgets are out of scope, as are reseller accounts and subscription charges such as Gemini Enterprise seats. The estimate that drives enforcement is calculated at list price and excludes credits and discounts. Enforcement is also not instant: the documentation states that "any cost overages are billed as normal" and advises setting the cap "slightly below your absolute limit." Compute Engine, Cloud Storage, and networking are not among the eligible services, which means the cap addresses a runaway model or agent loop and not a forgotten GPU instance.

The narrower scope drew criticism when the preview expanded in October. On a Hacker News thread, one commenter who had asked Google for the capability a year earlier to control AI spend called the result "very crude, not granular like the rich IAM system," noting the four-product, month-only design. Another observed that Cloud Run had been added that morning, so the service list was still growing.

Developer Simon Willison argued in October that pay-by-usage services should default to hard caps, because coding and personal agents make it far easier to spin up code that calls paid APIs, and that "most businesses and individuals would prefer errors to a surprise $10,000+ bill." He cited Google's caps alongside a project-level spend limit in AWS's new account experience, which pauses the whole project and stops its resources when reached, preserving data for 90 days, and which AWS is rolling out to a limited number of customers. Azure's spending limit remains confined to free accounts and credit-based subscriptions; Microsoft states it isn't available for subscriptions with commitment plans or with pay-as-you-go pricing.

Spend caps pair with early anomalies, a daily baseline model that flags directional cost variances per service and names the top SKUs behind a spike, which Google positions as the detection half of the same control loop. Both are configured from the Budgets and Alerts page in the Billing console or through the Budgets API.

InfoQ Homepage Articles LLM Reliability Layers Under Test: Accuracy, Yield, and the Limits of Verification

Nov 10, 2026 15 min read

Clinical notes, incident reports, support tickets, and forum posts are prose, and a language model can read them. What it produces is not dependable. Ours was right about one answer in three on the task below and reported full confidence on two-thirds of its answers; wherever a wrong answer costs something, that is unusable.

We tested six layers, checks wrapped around the model, that each evaluate the model's output and flag what is wrong so that it can be put right: check each answer against something deterministic, send provable failures back to the model [1], sample it several times and vote [2], have a second model judge it [3], withhold what cannot be corroborated, and send the rest to a person. The theory we set out to test is that these layers are additive: each one catches errors the layers below it missed, so accuracy rises with every layer added. That assumption is what justifies stacking them at all. We ran all six on the same records, each priced, with the free check entered as a competitor. The theory did not hold. The layers sorted the answers by how far to trust them and corrected almost none.

Microsoft Ignite 2026; Explore What's Next in AI and Software Engineering - Learn how to architect agentic systems, modernize cloud infrastructure, and tackle emerging engineering challenges. Get practical insights from Microsoft experts and industry leaders. Microsoft Ignite is less than two weeks away! Register now to join us in person or attend online for free, November 17–20.

Testing it needs a graded task, so we chose an annotated corpus: CADEC, the CSIRO Adverse Drug Event Corpus [4], 1,250 forum posts about two drugs, every adverse reaction marked as a span, the writer's exact words such as "rectal bleed", and given a SNOMED CT code. A gold answer for every mention lets us score each layer's verdicts instead of trusting them — and that assumes gold is the only right answer, which is not always true. Running one development document through the pipeline, the span "extremely sick" is gold-coded 213257006 |Generally unwell|; the model answered 39104002 |Illness| — an equally active, equally general reading that shares no wording with the span or with gold's own name. Naming either is a real answer; only one is scored correct. We did not build a CADEC system or tune for it: the aim was to evaluate open-weight models and measure how much each layer raises their accuracy, and CADEC is the instrument.

A supervised system does far better. CONORM [5], fine-tuned on 875 of CADEC's 1,250 files, reaches an end-to-end F1 of 0.72 under lenient span matching. Our zero-shot extractor, everything it returns, reaches 0.47 to 0.50 under the same matching on our development split, 0.39 to 0.43 span-exact. We are not competing with it: it needs those 875 annotated files, and still cannot say which of its answers to trust. Our held-out split was spent once: 60 documents, one run; every other number is development-side and says so. Two things would move the absolute numbers: the judge is small, and CADEC, public since 2015, is almost certainly in its training data. The comparisons between layers should hold.

The model is never asked for a code, and everything above it depends on that choice.

Figure 1: The pipeline, left to right, on one illustrative post (CADEC is non-transferable). Teal: a model call; grey: deterministic code. Each card shows what that step produces for the example; code appears for the first time in the last one. Image: the authors.

The model quotes each reaction; a retriever with no language model in it searches 227,554 keyword-to-code rows — every description in the SNOMED CT Australian edition of 31 July 2026 — embedding each quote with granite-embedding:30m, 384 dimensions, run locally, and showing the model a menu of the 20 nearest concepts to pick from. Codes are written as 271782001 |Drowsy|, SNOMED's bar notation, throughout.

We chose this shape by measuring the alternatives on 40 development documents, three cold runs each: recall the code from memory, name the concept and look it up, or pick from the menu.

Figure 2: F1 of the extractor alone under three ways of getting the code, one dot per cold run, with tokens per run and unparseable replies beneath. Image: the authors.

Recalling the code is not weak but broken: eight to twelve times worse than naming the concept. It answered null on up to 38 percent of records, and of the codes it committed to, 13 to 18 percent exist in no SNOMED release.

F1 is span-exact throughout: a wrong boundary is both a false positive and a false negative.

That is the extractor the six layers sit on. Before measuring what a layer adds, we had to know how much its output moves between runs of the same inputs.

Three identical runs of the unchanged extractor differed by four points of F1, at temperature zero.

We ran the extraction step three times, cold, on the same 40 documents. Two runs were byte-identical. The third diverged and finished with 98 correct mentions against the pair's 87. Grouped by mention, the runs give 233 mentions:

Where all three found the same span, they agreed on the code 84 percent of the time, so what moves between runs is mostly the reading, not the coding. We did not ask why those 22 were coded differently, and we should have.

The variation is a property of this model: four other families run three times each, llama3.1:8b, mistral:7b-instruct, qwen3:8b, and granite4:micro-h, returned byte-identical output every time. Our extractor, gpt-oss:20b, is the only mixture-of-experts model of the five and the only one that varied. Why, we could not settle: the diverging request, sent alone eight times, returns one reply; it moves only inside a full run. We kept it for its 6.5 extra points of F1 over llama3.1:8b.

From then on, every change was measured on three runs and reported as the range across them.

The two free layers did their jobs: the vocabulary check sorted the answers, and refusal withheld the doubtful ones, and neither changed an answer. Of the three paid layers, self-correction almost never fired, voting did not help, and the judge worked, but nothing read its verdict.

Development split, three runs of 40 documents; figures below are ranges across the runs:

Then we removed the three paid layers and replayed refusal: what it passed differed by one record on the first run and by none on the other two.

Voting and the judge's verdicts are good for diagnosis, not repair: a split vote marks an answer the model cannot reproduce, and a judge answering not on this menu marks a failed menu, not a failed pick. We did not use them that way, and should have.

Every verdict carries information. None makes the system produce more right answers, and they do not stack.

Figure 3: The first run's 230 records under each shipping rule. Dark green: right code on the exact span; light green: exact span, wrong code; light teal: right code, boundary off; grey: neither; amber: to a person. Dotted lines: the most the extractor found. Image: the authors.

Figure 3 reads each verdict as a shipping rule over the same 230 records. Three things hold.

First, any verdict raises the accuracy of what passes, because withholding is a layer's only lever. No rule ships more than the 88 right answers the extractor found, so every gain in accuracy is paid for in right answers held back. Accuracy alone flatters any withholding layer. Yield, right answers as a share of all records, cannot be flattered, so we report both.

Second, the price differs by verdict. ACCEPT is the most precise rule and the most expensive: moving to it removes 128 errors at the cost of 49 right answers and 177 records for a person, because the check can endorse only 32 percent of mentions whose wording matches the vocabulary. The menu-shown judge's verdict is informed rather than lexical, and it is the one rule whose F1 beats shipping everything, 0.420 against 0.397.

Third, the verdicts nest rather than add. Nearly everything the free check endorses, the judge endorses too, so a second verdict mostly confirms the first and can only remove records, never recover one the first held back; that is why removing the paid layers changed one record. Where every verdict agrees, the answers are almost all right; where only a vote does, almost all wrong. Each verdict is a different trade between errors, lost answers, and reviews; we recommend none.

The held-out split, run once, tells the same story. Before any check, one answer in three was right. Of those, the vocabulary check marked ACCEPT, five in six were right; of the rest, one in five. The check changed no answer; it only sorted them.

The domain knowledge was never missing. The model loses at reading, and nothing above it can see what it did not read.

Figure 4: Where the development split's 226 annotated mentions go through the shipped extractor, gpt-oss:20b, first run. Each bar is what the stage above kept; the dotted steps are what each stage lost. Teal: a model call; grey: deterministic code. Image: the authors.

Once a mention is found, the pipeline is reliable: the right concept reaches the menu for 93 percent of found spans and the model picks it 81 percent of the time. The loss is at finding. Nearly half the annotated mentions are never quoted as the annotators marked them, and none of the reasons are medical: single words go untouched where the annotator marked them, boundaries stretch — an extra adjective swept into a span the annotator left bare — and figures of speech are read literally, an idiom for exasperation coded as a nocturnal behaviour. Two domain-adapted models did not help: BioMistral-7B could not reliably return a parseable reply, and SapBERT as the retriever's encoder improved the menu and worsened the answer. A worked example and a denied-reactions rule did.

No layer can add a mention. Every layer above the extractor checks, re-asks, votes on, judges, or withholds an answer the extractor already gave; none can find a mention it missed. So the most any stack can ever get right is what the extractor found in the first place: on the held-out split, just over half the annotated mentions, a detection F1 of 0.521. The one way past that ceiling is a second reader: a different model family shown the post and the first model's spans, asked what was missed. We did not build it. Its additions would be proposals, needing the same measurement as the extractor's.

Four of the five lessons were held on other data. What changed is where the model loses, and where the free check can be trusted.

Seven further corpora [6–10], five model families; for each lesson, does the data agree?

The judge, the nesting of verdicts, and the diagnostic use of split votes were measured on CADEC only.

Every layer passed its own tests. The two defects that mattered sat between the layers and inside the metric.

The first was the unread verdicts. The second was the metric: voting overwrote codes without re-running the vocabulary check, so records shipped marked verified for a code they no longer held. Fixing that moved the held-out F1 from 0.204 to 0.204, because precision and recall cannot tell an unwarranted answer from a wrong one. We built six layers to decide which answers to trust, then scored them with a metric that cannot see the difference.

Both defects share a shape: a load-bearing fact recorded once, so nothing could disagree with it. Three checks built from that lesson, gatecheck, crosscheck and stagecheck, are reached from the repository below; stagecheck is a standalone package with no dependencies.

What transfers is not the layers but six practices, each earned above.

We set out believing that stacking reliability layers buys reliability. Measured end to end, they sorted the answers by how far to trust them and fixed almost none: worth a great deal, and not what we bought them for. The model reads. Almost everything else belongs to code, and one choice to you.

Code, ledger, decision records, and every figure's source. CADEC is non-transferable; we ship document IDs, never text.

InfoQ Homepage Presentations Batch Intelligence at Scale: Cost-Efficient Multi-Agent LLM Workflows with Built-In Resilience

Aditya Mulik explains how Walmart Global Tech deployed multi-agent LLM workflows across 4,700 stores for nightly batch inventory forecasting. He discusses tackling production hurdles - schema violations, cross-department data mismatches, and network drops - via MCP grounding and loop agents, while slashing cloud inference costs from $100k+ to $10k per month shifting from PTUs to pay-as-you-go.

Aditya Mulik is a Senior Software Engineer at Walmart Global Tech specializing in large-scale distributed systems and platform engineering, with deep expertise in deploying scalable and resilient solutions. He has led the development of microservices architectures and production LLM systems, enabling high-throughput applications serving millions of requests. He also contributes to open source.

QCon AI is a practitioner-led event focused entirely on the engineering discipline required to scale these workloads safely. It provides direct access to the architectural playbooks and failure metrics that peer organizations use in production.

Presented by: David Iparraguirre - Senior Product Marketing Manager at Datadog, and Jessica Yang - Product Manager at Datadog

Presented by: Kiran Gangadharappa - Engineering Advisor at Harness, and Scott Davis - Senior Engineering Manager at Harness

Aditya Mulik: My topic today is, Batch Intelligence at Scale, which is, Cost-Efficient Multi-Agent LLM Workflows with Built-In Resilience. My name is Aditya Mulik. I work as a senior software engineer at Walmart Global Tech.

The problem I want to walk you through today is one which all retailers are wrestling with right now, which is inventory accuracy. What I want to say is the retail at massive scale, so Walmart has around 4,700 stores within the U.S. They have multiple stores in Canada, Mexico, and so on as well, but the major chunk is in the U.S. itself. There are hundreds of thousands of SKUs. There are varying departments, like varying products, any product that we can imagine of, those are all available at Walmart. The physical space is also quite large and so many items have to be tracked very carefully. The problem that each retailer is dealing with, as I mentioned about inventory accuracy, is where we have to determine what is the right inventory required for a particular occasion. If it's Thanksgiving, we have to make sure that much inventory is available at a particular point in time.

What I want to mention over here is, can we predict what inventory a department needs? I'm particularly looking for a particular department here. Can we predict what inventory a department needs for the next n number of days accurately, automatically, and at scale? Because the scale is quite large, and if you are targeting a particular department, it has to have a particular set of items available. If you are walking towards a shelf and if that shelf doesn't have a particular item available, it's not a good experience. We have to ensure that each item has to be available and in stock.

The accuracy imperative. Accuracy isn't a nice-to-have, it's very mission critical, on any particular item. What we worked on was fresh food, or basically the items, you can take an example like rotisserie chicken or fresh beef, these items can expire very quickly. The store manager has to decide very accurately like, what items are there in stock and what items need to be ordered, to make sure that the inventory is very accurate for the next n number of days. The line between a stock shelf and an empty rack is what we are looking over here right now. If there are stockouts or overstock, then it could be a problem. If it's an overstock, obviously the items are going to go to waste. Either it has to be donated or it has to be thrown away. Consider this compounded across millions of square feet of retail. We have 4,700 stores, so you can imagine how critical this could be for a particular store. The next thing I'll say here is, how do we keep inventory accurate at this scale? Our first answer has to be an obvious one, we have to forecast the demand based on the history.

Now the thing is like, in Walmart, we already have machine learning models which can actually predict the amount of inventory that has to be ordered. We have backward-looking machine learning models which are trained on historical sales data to forecast future demand. We use like a year's or maybe two years of sales data, where we are trying to determine, ok, this much amount of inventory has to be ordered for the next maybe 30 days or maybe next 60 days, and so on. It's reliable for steady, recurring, and seasonal trends. Walmart is very focused on Thanksgiving. We know during Thanksgiving we have to order n number of items. That is very accurate for prediction. It's fully automated and it scales across SKUs and stores. The problem right now is sales forecasting accuracy. The pro over here is like, based on the historical trends, it can get it properly.

What if there are blind unprecedented events. Consider an example where there's a storm coming. Now the machine learning model won't be able to accurately determine if there's a storm, and if we need water or certain other items. Will that be actually available well in advance? That machine learning model might not be able to predict that. These are the things that the store manager has to deal with. They can't just rely on this particular backward trend. The question became, how do we add real-time, real-world context on top of the signals we already trust. The thing is, since we already have the machine learning models and we already know the prediction can be done in a specific way, this was an engineering challenge that came on to us, where we had to determine how should we just not rely on backward trends. Like, this is something that's going to happen, and then this number of stock has to be ordered. What if we can add some real-time context to it?

That's where we came up with a multi-agent — not monolith — basically a sequential workflow of agents where this agentic flow can actually determine, like get the real-time data along with some forecasting model data as well, and get the exact accurate decision that has to be given to the store manager, where these are the number of items that they need to order. How did we do that? Basically, in the sequential agentic workflow, we added multi-agent orchestration, like one agent after another, where first agent would determine, so in this case, it gathers context from various data sources for a particular department. Consider if it's a department, or like as I said about the fresh food, it's getting all the metadata from various sources like BigQuery and so on. That context is then fed to the agent, which outputs more accurate data. If it's like rotisserie chicken, it has to be ordered for the quantity that needs to be determined.

Then the second agent consumes that data, and then it synthesizes past sales data prepared by the data engineering pipeline. We have another pipeline which is set to get more context. What it does is, there's an amalgamation of two datasets which is collated, and then it is fed to another third agent data. Now this agent is quite important, which analyzes the context and output from the previous agent, and there is a grounding mechanism over here now. Consider, as I said, just a backward ML trend is not going to get us the exact prediction. There might be a weather agent, or there might be some weather API call. There could be a trending event that's happening, a sales event is happening over there. It can gather that context and it can make more accurate predictions, now that it has to order more or either less of the inventory. Then, finally, the fourth agent which produces the final natural language recommendation directive, which is like, increase the stock by this percent, and so on. That's the entire sequential workflow that we created. This was particularly targeted for a specific department.

Going to the next part, so the output, and this is the human-in-the-loop. What we were trying to do over here was, all this context generation and everything was fine, but our only goal was the store manager has to get a clear directive. The clear directive was not a dashboard, it was just a clear command. Increase apple stock 15% in produce ahead of the weekend. They are having a clear context on what they need to do. It's not just that, they are just going to increase the stock right away. There's a real-time data integration as I mentioned, which is like, MCP lets agents pull live data sources during reasoning. All of that is verified using live tools, MCPs, and so on, which is grounded properly. The critical checkpoint is, the store manager reviews each recommendation, and the store manager can either accept it or override it, because they will manually go and check and verify if there is actually a shortage of apples, if they do want to order it. There was a human in the loop in this entire pattern. This is a feature not a fallback. Human oversight in the final validation layer was catching the local context that the data cannot. That was the additional validation that the store manager was doing.

Now this is where the important aspect comes. Then we went to production. The pilot was magic, because we tested it on maybe 10 stores or 15 stores, and then we had a clear output, coherent recommendations, and the stakeholders were quite happy about it. Then we flipped the switch to full-scale nightly runs. This was not like a real-time context where the store manager is communicating with the app, it's just a recommendation that the store manager gets during the start of the day. As Walmart stores open in the early morning, they get this recommendation and then they act upon it. Yes, we did this. We went to full chain, like all 4,700-plus stores that we rolled it out to. Then, what works at the scale of one rarely survives the scale of thousands. Here are the four failures that taught us why. Because we saw that, ok, we went to production, we made it like full chain available.

Initially for a 10-store pilot, recommendations were great, we got good feedback. There were a lot of engineering teams, data science team getting involved, and they had everything analyzed. The main thing was, me and my team's task was to get it to production, and then we started observing a lot of failures. There were four main failures which taught us what exactly happened.

I'm moving on towards the failures now. These are the common failures that we have always seen in the LLM world, and this is how we also tackled it. The very first one was hallucinations and schema violations. Here as you can see, hallucinations and schema violations were like, we had to enforce structured outputs, because the agentic workflow was getting the context, it was getting all the data, the output as well. The output was vague at times, because it was not always validated how it has to be given to the end user. Here you can see like we had to add some schema layer, which is basically to validate outputs via some JSON format of identical validation. I can give a bad example output over here. This was one of the recommendations that went to one of the store managers. As you can see over here, I think you should probably increase some stock for the weekend, maybe around 15% or so, for produce items that sell well.

It's like a coherent recommendation. It doesn't make sense, because it says that you have to increase the stock, but what stock? We don't know. Is it apples? Is it rotisserie chicken? What is it? We don't know. It's just saying that around 15%, and it should be about something that's in the produce section. Now the store manager has to go and verify which is the item that's low on stock. This didn't make sense, and it still gave a pretty high confidence. The thing is it did validate across the schema but it didn't make sense from a good output perspective. What we did is we fixed it with structured output, but also making sure that the item is also validated across a specific SKU, whether it's like which department it belongs to. If it's a produce, the item name has to be there, which is the Honeycrisp Apple right over there, and the SKU.

This confidence level made sure like this is adhering to the department that it has to be. This can be extended as possible. The stock directive, the schema which is given, and this is all validated across a common schema. As I mentioned, here's the same output. Now schema enforced, every field typed and validated: the exact SKU, item, department, and adjustment. No ambiguity left. This was the first failure.

Then, again, now the second failure. This is something that we encountered once we were full chain as well, where it was semantically wrong. What do we mean by that? You're ensuring data integrity with semantic validation. Even though we did have structured output there was a problem. The problem was the data engineering, because the workflow that was created was an overnight job, where we gathered data from various sources. Walmart has so much data for each produce, or each department. Once we pull the data, there were certain things that were getting mapped properly but certain things were not getting mapped accurately. This was a one-off situation maybe out of hundreds of items that we found out there was an issue. I can give an example over here. As you see here, everything is matching to the schema, but as we can see I have highlighted over here.

At Walmart there are multiple departments. The SKUs are unique as well, but the problem is it can map one of the items which has the same item number, but the department is different. The other department might have the same item number. The only varying part is the department over here. If there's a product that's coming as Honeycrisp Apple, but it is coming from auto_parts, so there should be some recommendation for auto_parts which was showing to the end user, but for an auto_part it was showing as a Honeycrisp Apple, which doesn't make sense. This was a failure at the data ingestion layer, where basically, wherever the data was being fetched from, it was not correctly being mapped. We had to also validate if the SKU is correct and also the item name is also coming from the same SKU. This was one of the failures. It was a dangerous kind of failure, perfectly valid JSON, but it maps Honeycrisp Apple to auto_parts because the SKUs collided.

The schema can't catch this, only schema validation can. How did we ensure to fix this? This is the part where we did the grounding mechanism. As you can see, we introduced an MCP tool call over here, basically validate coherence, where if you are getting a stock directive, we have an MCP client which is connecting to the proper data source. From that proper data source, we are looking up the proper catalog that belongs to that item. We made sure that any item that is not relevant to that particular data pipeline, it should not be processed as well. That's where we ensured, ok, this is something that every output coming from an agent has to be validated with some grounding mechanism. This was another failure that we encountered.

Then there was one more. This is the place where it's very common, where outputs were vanished. What do I mean by that? Over here you can see unreliable inference, which is failure disrupts perfect inferences, and then there is resilient inference. What I mean by that is there could be network issues, like network partition can happen as we know. Here you can see we had some logs enabled. Because in the pipeline, the way it was, there are multiple agents doing multiple tasks, and then their task was to just save the output to some persistent storage. What if there is a db_write failure, like here you can see clearly there is a timeout error which is the connection reset that has happened. Because of the connection reset the output has just vanished now. We didn't have a mechanism initially where if the output is vanished, maybe there are like 4700 stores, maybe some of the stores were not even saving the output and the store manager was just opening the app, and seeing like, ok, there's no recommendation today, that means everything is proper.

That was a failure that was observed. How did we fix that as well? This is where the mechanism of the agentic workflow agent comes over here. We had a sequential agent, and we then determined to let's switch it to a loop agent. As you can see, the main thing that I'm highlighting over here is the max iterations. These are the bounded retries that we have added. If there are any sort of failures happening, the agentic workflow will ensure to retry. Right now, you can see there are multiple sub-agents over here, like data gathering agent, synthesis agent, grounding agent. We add an iteration for the entire loop. This might be not that cost efficient, but maybe we can add this max iteration to a particular agent as well where the main work is handled. In this case, I'm showing the entire agent can make sure the workflow is computed properly, so that the output is saved and you can increase the number of iterations as much as you want. Like here, I've just given as an example of five iterations.

Next one, so we were flying blind. Observability is a must. This is a very important thing. Since we had full chain available, the very common thing that we want to ensure is we should have observability in the entire workflow. What do I mean by that? The regular way of doing the engineering is you can have maybe 5xx errors or 4xx errors. Even here it's the same case, like if we saw like there's a schema violation, or if there are retries that didn't happen, or something that went wrong, we were adding structured logging. Structured logging was ensured. Over here you can see, as an example, is how many times the retries happened, like two attempts. Or if there was a schema violation, how much latency it had. All of this was enabled just to make sure that we have clear visibility into what's happening in our entire workflow.

As you can see over here, we had proper logging if the LLM response doesn't match the schema, if there is a tool response that failed, so it was retrying again. We were trying to reach a success rate of 99%, so this is some example that I added, like if there's a 97% error rate, 2.3% of failure rate, and so on. We made sure that observability is also in place.

That being said, these were the four main failures. Now I want to get to the very crisp part of what we were building over here. With failures behind us, the next question on the table was cost. This was the biggest question that was running on to us, because we wanted to get this into production. This was a very critical thing that we were trying to get in, but we were leveraging all foundational models, and with a high scale, like 4700 stores, the amount of tokens generated is quite high. How much of this whole solution is going to cost to run, is what was the next question. The thing over here is the PTU problem. When the solution went to production, we had humongous amount of input and output tokens being used. Since it was a foundational model, we decided to secure 400 PTUs for that amount of scale that we need to run.

For 400 PTUs, we were almost being charged around 100k to 120k per month, and the solution was not that, because we are trying to also make sure that the inventory is accurate, also save cost. There should not be wastage happening in a particular department. Running at this position, it doesn't make sense, if these much PTUs are going to be used. PTUs is like reserved capacity, and we have to reserve it for the entire day. Any of these vendors who are providing the PTUs, we have to take it for the entire 24 hours, and it's a monthly or yearly subscription that we have to take in. Now the problem is, this is a batch intelligence application, it's only going to run overnight. It's not going to run the entire day. We are not serving any live traffic. For us, we were being charged 400 PTUs for 24 hours, and PTUs reserve capacity for the entire day.

Our workload was nightly batch run, which was not 24 by 7. We were paying for the round the clock capacity which was being used. This was our most critical problem that we were trying to solve in a particular defined set of time, which didn't make sense because of the cost involved. We started exploring options, what are the options? Because this is a foundational model, the best part about foundational model is they are well trained, they are good for such enterprise scale applications. The next thing that came to us was, what AI model should I choose? Here as you can see, we had cheaper models where we can go with open-source models or any other models, which might introduce some latency, or they could be cheaper compared to the one which we already had, whether it's a GPT-4.1 or Gemini, and so on. Or we can just reduce the PTUs, that was another option. Or we could go with pay-as-you-go. We were debating between quality versus cost, and both were not a good option, because we needed high quality but lower cost. That was the main problem that we had to solve.

How did we do that? The decision that we made was, we went with pay-as-you-go. Now just to go with some background, the problem with PTU versus pay-as-you-go, is pay-as-you-go will come with latency issues, as I've mentioned over here, and there will be rate limiting happening. We did observe a lot of rate limit issues, but now the thing is, as I mentioned in my failures as well, we had added a retry logic. This retry logic enabled us, even if there is a rate limit, if there are any 4xx or 5xx errors, I'm just going to retry again. If I do retry again, the output will be generated again. Now, if it's a pay-as-you-go, it's still a foundational model and it still gives very accurate responses, and there was no reserved capacity as well over here. We were not paying round the clock, we were just using as much compute as we require.

The payoff, so what happened over here is, we were supposed to pay around 100k per month, now we are paying just 10k as the final usage, because even with the retries, the cost is just very low. It's just going to happen in that specific 30 minutes, or maybe the time might increase. We don't mind because the stores are already closed, this is going to be a nightly job. Then, eventually, whatever compute is being taken is going to just happen at night, even if there are retries and everything, the cost will be minimal, output will be still matching the quality that we have set, because there is schema enforcement, and all the data engineering pipelines were fixed. This was our biggest win. As I mentioned, same tokens, same model, just a pricing tier switch from PTU to pay-as-you-go, and for a drastic drop in cost. That was the big win that I wanted to mention over here.

Every failure and every fix left us with a lesson worth carrying forward. Here's what we learned, building at scale. These are some of the learnings that I'm going to mention once again. Basically, schema enforcement. Since we are retrying, everything is all planned out, but schema enforcement is not just the one thing that happens over here, basic JSON validation. There has to be semantic validation, because data, what is coming from various sources, like in the AI domain, what I've learned very well is context has to be very accurate. With the data pipelines that we have, whatever context we get, if there is even a slight mismatch, it can just return vague output. We have to make sure semantic validation is in place. Data usability, making data easy to understand and use. We need to have some observability as well. Whatever the data that is coming in, we need to see how the pipelines are running.

All those things have to be taken into consideration. Then comes the data integrity. Maintaining accuracy and consistency of data is very important, is what I've learned from this entire perspective. That's one of the key features. Second thing is data engineering limits AI quality. As I mentioned over here, data quality over here is reliable AI results, accurate and trustworthy outcomes, data rectification, fixing upstream data issues, data engineering, ensuring data quality, and flawed data. What do we mean by all of this? The entire context over here, since it was a nightly job that was being run, this had a huge emphasis on the data engineering aspects itself. Because we were getting data from various sources, there was a lot of focus that was added into this perspective, where data coming from various sources has to be verified. The amount of scripts that we created were validated with the business and the data team, with the engineering team.

We ensured that once the data is very accurate, then that context will be fed properly to the agents with a proper particular prompt, and that will generate the exact output that we are looking for. Because it's a multi-agent workflow, we have to ensure that the data that is coming from various sources has to be very accurate. That was our main goal that we learned from this entire workflow. Also, like flawed data, as I mentioned, unreliable input is also like, once the input is not proper, all the agents will react in a weird output, because it might either not match to the schema, or it might just match to the schema also, but just generate vague outputs, and that's how it would return. Building retry logic, this was one of the most important aspects that I went through. We have been very much emphasizing on this.

Retries can be a very bad loop as well, where if you don't plan it properly, it can just keep retrying, and that would just increase a lot of costs. Just because there are tokens involved over here, you have to plan it very accurately. As I mentioned in the initial flow, we used to retry it at the base, like root level, where all the agents are, like where the workflow runs. Ideally, the agentic retry should be in such a pattern where it is actually needed, whether we are getting the context from the proper data source, so that the further agents don't have to hallucinate, or there could be something that can go wrong. Retry over here, implemented from the outset, reduced unexpected failures. There's proactive system enhancement, cost saving and efficiency. It results into better user satisfaction.

The next learning perspective is observability. This is one of the key things from the entire workflow, that everyone is focused on. Traceability is important, structured logging is important, and enhanced system understanding. Because once you have tracing enabled, for us, initially, when we were having this entire workflow running, we were not able to determine, ok, what's going wrong, just because there are no proper traces set up. If you're not able to identify if the LLM is hallucinating, or if the model is just getting rate limited, all that has to be clearly available. There are tools which help us add those visibility, and then having that in place, make sure that your application is very robust, and can survive any failures to make it very resilient. That's a very important aspect that I'll mention over here. The next thing is matching infrastructure to requirements. This is the key thing that we realized, which is the infrastructure should be used for a nightly batch job.

Here, we can see that the real-time infrastructure, like if you have a real-time infrastructure where users are having conversations with the LLM, in that case, you can have an accurate set of PTUs set up, and so on. In cost effective infrastructures, you need to ensure that you don't need to actually have PTUs only allocated. Here, in our case, we were opting either for like pay-as-you-go, or you can even go with open-source models. As long as it's giving you the proper performance, the correct output, that's the ideal approach that you should be going with. The next one is, I've reached the end, which is, tomorrow at 6 a.m., a store manager will confidently adjust the inventory. They don't know about our schema validation, our loop retries, our nightly API optimizations. They don't have to as well. It's just the ultimate metric, which is the resilient engineering. That's what the perspective is.

Just to recap over here, schema is our table stakes, semantics is the real work. Focus on validation checks that go beyond basic JSON structure. Data engineering limits AI quality, so garbage in results into garbage out as well. Having retry logic is very important. If there are network timeouts, hallucinations, retry can ensure that your output is always accurate. Observability is mandatory. Tracing, logging is very important. Then match infrastructure to the regular requirements. If it's a nightly non-latency sensitive batch job, then you have to ensure that you don't need to actually rely on PTUs, because that would just cost you even more than what's expected. That's the main goal.

Participant 1: How did you assess the benefits or effectiveness of the new model compared to your legacy time series forecasting models? You said before you built this agentic flow, you had some forecasting models, the classical ML time series models. How did you assess whether this new agentic model was better?

Aditya Mulik: The thing is, basically, we had like time series forecast model, what we were using in this use case, because we had a lot of sales data. We were looking at the past two years of sales data for the forecasting model. Now that the forecasting model can just output in an accurate pattern for a fixed set of events, if it's a Thanksgiving event, the time series forecasting model will accurately determine what is needed. What if there is a situation where there's a flash sale happening somewhere, or there's a competitor having a flash sale, or consider if there is a certain situation, like a storm is coming. Then now the forecasting model won't be able to determine that. This is where we wanted to introduce agentic workflows. The entire goal was we have to create an output, but we wanted some real-time data mechanism as well. That's where we decided to go with the agentic workflow. That's how we decided to get the output here.

Participant 2: Did you keep the log only in testing, in the trial phase, or what about in production? Do you still keep all the logs in production?

Aditya Mulik: For production, you mentioned?

Participant 2: Did you keep all the logs in production?

Participant 2: We understand you write down all the logs, you check all the logs, during the testing it will help you to analyze what's wrong. Did you keep all the logs in the production phase?

Aditya Mulik: How did we ensure logging is maintained in production? There are multiple tools that were being used over here. Initially, we opted with cloud vendors themself, where they have their own logging tools and mechanism. We had our own custom logs added to every agentic workflow. Each agent that had custom functions, like as I mentioned in one of the MCP tools, where it was validating across one of the SKUs. In that SKU, we were trying to determine, like we were adding some logs, if there is a failure happening, or if there is any db_writes that's happening or db_reads happening. If there are any failures, we added custom logs, we had our own dashboards. Initially, we went with the cloud vendor dashboards, but we had our own custom dashboards to verify that.

Participant 3: At this conference, we had multiple sessions about evaluating the agents. I think for your case, it's especially interesting to hear, given it's deployed in production on many users, if you do changes to your agent, how do you evaluate it? How do you do regression testing and evaluations?

Aditya Mulik: This is the thing that I didn't include in my presentation as well, because this was our initial workflow where we went with an MVP. Because initially, our process was to come with an MVP, how should we determine the inventory accuracy. The things that we didn't include were evals. We did work upon in an iterative fashion where we had a certain perspective, like if there is a particular department, in this case, as I mentioned about rotisserie chicken, the shelf life is about 24 hours to 48 hours, is what I know about. There should be a certain eval set for particular departments as well. What we did initially was to go with a flow where we are getting real-time data. Once we have that data coming in, then we were able to iteratively add evals and a grounding mechanism, and even governance. Because at times, they were like, we had to make sure there are pricing information and everything that should not be coming in. All that was considered in an evaluation set. That's how we had approached it. That was not one of the perspectives that I did include, because that's something that was iteratively performed.

Participant 4: You mentioned that the store manager needs to act on the recommendation, either agree or override. Is there a possibility that the store manager does not take any action? Does the recommendation become stale because the next nightly run might have different parameters, and that might be a different recommendation.

Aditya Mulik: The thing is, the store managers didn't act upon it right away. The way it was done was initially when we were in the pilot phase, this was all being validated very thoroughly. The store manager has the ability to first go because it's kind of like their day-to-day process, they have their own applications where they're verifying the inventory that's coming in. This was just as a recommendation that was initially being shared to their store manager. They were manually validating it. As this went to full production, and over the iterative process of weeks and weeks, they were able to determine that the predictions were becoming more and more accurate, because that was actually the context that they were getting. The only thing was there was always a human in the loop perspective over here. Because even if the recommendation has come in, they won't just take the action right away, they will verify the inventory and then only they'll take a call. That's how it was done. It's not that they always acted upon it.

Participant 5: You mentioned the cost aspect. How did business decide in the end, is it worthwhile, given the cost to roll it out globally, or is there a tradeoff to take into account?

Aditya Mulik: This was an initial POC that we started off with. The way it was working out was initially recommendations were just based on machine learning models themselves. That's how it was running from a longer time. As this agentic workflow started coming through, and then we did already have something which was planned out to have this rolled out, like to certain pilot stores. The plan was initially to see how this fits in the entire ecosystem, because we didn't want to disturb the existing flow, because as the stores are operating right now, they're well efficient. This is just an additional layer that we added over here, just to make sure like, ok, this is getting more accurate recommendations. Then it went out as a POC, POC to the entire production, like 4700 stores. Then it was foolproof as a concept that got deployed. We iteratively kept adding more and more recommendations. Our initial plan was to do it for a specific department itself. Then that's how we scaled it up.

Presented by: Kiran Gangadharappa - Engineering Advisor at Harness, and Scott Davis - Senior Engineering Manager at Harness

InfoQ Homepage News Pinterest and Intel Take Open-Source Profiling to Internet Scale

Nov 10, 2026 3 min read

Pinterest has partnered with Intel to extend gProfiler, an open-source continuous profiler that provides production performance visibility across an internet-scale fleet of infrastructure.

Pinterest claims it operates hundreds of thousands of instances supporting 640 million monthly active users, with services written in Java, Python, C++, Go, and Node.js. At that scale, even a small efficiency improvement can have significant infrastructure and cost implications. The challenge was that engineers were using separate tools such as perf, async-profiler, and py-spy, producing fragmented profiles that were difficult to correlate across languages and machines.

Rather than building another proprietary profiling platform, Pinterest worked with Intel to extend gProfiler and contribute those capabilities back to the open-source project. The collaboration adds dynamic profiling, hardware-level telemetry, wall-time profiling, and performance-counter flame graphs, while also hardening the system for large production fleets.

gProfiler acts as an orchestration layer over several existing profilers. It combines system-wide perf data with runtime-specific tools such as async-profiler for Java and eBPF-based profiling for Python, producing a unified flame graph across the processes running on a host.

This is important for modern services where application execution crosses multiple layers. A Java application may spend time inside the JVM, native libraries, or the Linux kernel, while a Python service can involve interpreter, native, and kernel activity. Instead of requiring engineers to select and manually correlate separate profilers, gProfiler combines those views.

The agent discovers processes, starts the appropriate profilers, merges their stack traces, and uploads the resulting profile without requiring application code changes.

The new capability allows engineers to select services, hosts, or clusters and trigger profiling remotely through the UI or API. Agents normally remain dormant, consuming less than 1% CPU and 100 MB of memory, and receive profiling instructions through periodic heartbeats. You can change profiling frequency, duration, and the profilers used without redeploying the agent.

This effectively turns profiling into an operational capability rather than something that requires engineers to gain shell access to individual machines.

Integration with Intel PerfSpect allows gProfiler to collect hardware-level information such as cache behaviour, memory bandwidth, and CPU stalls. Pinterest also added PMC flame graphs, which correlate functions with hardware performance events such as cache misses and branch mispredictions.

Another addition is wall-time profiling. CPU profiling shows where processor time is being spent, but a service can still be slow while consuming relatively little CPU because threads are waiting on I/O, locks, or remote procedure calls. Wall-time profiling makes those waiting periods visible.

The gProfiler architecture separates collection from analysis. Agents run on individual hosts, while gProfiler Performance Studio aggregates and visualises the resulting profiles.

Profiles are stored in S3, passed through SQS for asynchronous indexing, and stored in ClickHouse for analytical queries. A React frontend provides flame-graph exploration, while aggregated data allows engineers to compare profiles across hosts, services, and time periods.

Pinterest and Intel also hardened the platform for production, addressing issues involving agent resource consumption, disk usage, and multi-terabyte ingestion before deploying it across the fleet.

gProfiler sits in an increasingly crowded continuous-profiling landscape. Parca and Grafana Pyroscope use eBPF and related sampling technologies to provide fleet-wide profiling, while commercial platforms such as Datadog offer continuous profiling alongside broader observability capabilities.

The distinction with gProfiler is its emphasis on system-wide, multi-runtime profiling and open-source extensibility. Rather than replacing tools such as perf or async-profiler, it orchestrates them. It also adds hardware-level profiling through Intel's tooling.

Intel's VTune provides much deeper processor and application performance analysis, but it was designed primarily as a performance-engineering tool rather than a cloud-native fleet profiler. Pinterest specifically notes that this difference influenced the decision to build on gProfiler rather than adapt VTune for its environment.

At small scale, profiling is usually something an engineer enables when a performance problem appears. At Pinterest's scale, the cost of waiting for engineers to reproduce problems or manually profile machines becomes too high. Continuous profiling provides a baseline, while dynamic profiling allows teams to investigate specific anomalies when they occur.

By combining runtime profiles, system behaviour, and hardware performance data under an open-source platform, Pinterest is effectively making profiling another layer of production observability.

Find the bugs your tests miss. Antithesis uses deterministic simulation to explore thousands of failure scenarios, uncovering hard-to-reproduce bugs before they reach production. See how it works.

Attackers Crash Claude's 'Office Party' Flaw to Run Malicious Code on Linux

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Attackers can exploit a flaw in Claude's document-processing tool by creating a temporary file that Anthropic's AI assistant can access and run on a user's machine.

An attacker can use a flaw in Anthropic Claude's document-processing tool to run malicious code on Linux systems just by creating a temporary file that the AI assistant can access. The flaw highlights a growing risk posed by even trusted AI tools to introduce security gaps in enterprise environments.

Researchers from AIR Security discovered the flaw, which they've dubbed "Office Party," in Anthropic's official DOCX, PPTX, and XLSX skills, which are tools that enable Claude to create, read, edit, and process Microsoft Word documents, PowerPoint presentations, and Excel spreadsheets, respectively.

The vulnerability stems from a helper script, soffice.py, that launches LibreOffice to convert documents, including rendering Word documents as PDFs, they revealed in a report published today. When the script encounters an environment where Unix-domain sockets cannot be opened, it uses a workaround that loads a shared library through the Linux LD_PRELOAD mechanism.

Related:Google, Partners Disrupt 1.3 Million-Device-Strong XCPE Botnet

Anthropic has opted not to fix the issue.

During conversation, the script checks whether a library named lo_socket_shim.so already exists in a shared temporary directory but does not verify its origin or contents. An attacker who can place a malicious library at that location before the next document-processing operation can potentially have their code loaded and executed within LibreOffice, with the privileges of the user running the AI agent.

In this way, the flaw can allow attackers to execute malicious code on a user's Linux machine by exploiting an unverified file path used during document conversion, explains Niv Hoffman, co-founder and chief technology officer (CTO) of AIR Research Lab, in an email interview.

"An attacker gets one malicious file into a shared temporary folder which many programs use, and the next time an employee asks Claude for a document, Anthropic's trusted skill loads that file and runs it," he tells Dark Reading.

The vulnerability affects Linux environments where Unix sockets are blocked, including certain container security configurations, and Claude Code's sandbox. AIR Security reports that macOS is not affected because it does not load the library through LD_PRELOAD.

The AIR researchers demonstrated two ways the attack can work on Linux systems. In one scenario, they manually placed a malicious library in the temporary directory before asking Claude Code to generate a document. In another, they used a separate, seemingly legitimate AI skill to unpack a supporting file into the same directory. When the document-processing skill subsequently ran, the malicious library was loaded automatically.

Related:'Aokigahara' Stealer Targets 'Developer Keyrings' & AI Assistants

The flaw presents a serious issue for enterprises running Linux because it exploits "the default skills that all Claude agents use — [of which there are] hundreds of millions — and one planted file can make them run an attacker’s code with the employee's own access," Hoffman explains.

This second attack scenario is particularly concerning for enterprise security teams because it illustrates how individually benign-looking AI skills can interact to create an attack chain. And often, "companies don't have visibility to which skills and other agent add-ons they have installed, and don't have a red button to revoke or patch a vulnerable one," Hoffman explains.

Moreover, a malicious or compromised skill does not necessarily need to exploit the document-processing tool directly; it may simply need to place a file that another trusted component will execute, according to the AIR researchers.

The AIR team discovered the flaw in May and reported it to Anthropic, which acknowledged its existence, according to the report. However, the AI company "considers it outside its security boundary, since the vulnerable code only runs inside a sandbox," AIR Research Lab's Omri Saxena and Or Nevo wrote in the report. This means that as of now, the helper script still uses the same fixed path and still loads whatever file it finds there, they said.

Related:BeyondTrust Remote Support Zero-Day Continues Trend of Exploited Edge Bugs

Hoffman says that Anthropic's response is "not quite" adequate, "because even if the skills run within a sandbox, the attacker’s code then runs inside it with the user's own permissions, where the user's files and work already are," he says. Anthropic did not immediately return Dark Reading's request for comment about whether the company would take any further action to remedy the issue.

For businesses deploying AI agents and coding assistants, Office Party reinforces the need to treat AI skills as software dependencies rather than trusted extensions, and secure them as such, according to AIR.

To mitigate, organizations should make it a point to know "exactly what skills or add-ons an organization is using with Claude Code by keeping a live inventory of every AI skill and add-on across employees' agents," Hoffman says. They also should "review even official ones like any other code, and update or patch the whole set whenever a new vulnerability is published."

Elizabeth Montalbano is freelance writer, editor, and journalist with 30 years of professional experience and a master's degree from Arizona State University. Her areas of expertise include enterprise technology, cybersecurity, business, and culture. During her long career, Elizabeth has lived and worked as a full-time journalist in Phoenix, San Francisco, and New York City. She specializes in news coverage and analysis, using her years of experience to look at the current state of cybersecurity with a critical gaze. She currently resides in a village on the southwest coast of Portugal, where in her free time she enjoys surfing, hiking with her dogs, growing plants, and playing and performing as a singer and musician.

Black Hat India 2026 Makes a Landmark Debut in Bengaluru, Bringing the Global Cybersecurity Community to India

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Inaugural edition by Informa Markets in India brings together leading researchers, security practitioners and industry decision makers for four days of cutting-edge research, technical exchange and strategic dialogue.

13 Trainings delivered by 19 trainers, 70+ Summit speakers, 200+ CXOs, CISOs and VIP buyers, and 2,000+ Business Hall delegates across four days.

Black Hat Arsenal featured 44 open-source security tool demonstrations across 32 workstations and four hands-on labs.

Keynotes by leading cybersecurity experts Dr. Sanjay Bahl, Director General, Indian Computer Emergency Response Team (CERT-In) and Controller of Certifying Authorities (CCA), and Nathan Hamiel (Kudelski Security), alongside a special address by Shri Rajesh Kumar, IPS, Chief Executive Officer, Indian Cybercrime Co-ordination Centre (I4C), Ministry of Home Affairs, Government of India.

Key themes included AI security, digital payment fraud, emerging cyber threats and post-quantum readiness.

Bengaluru, November 5, 2026 — Black Hat India 2026, organised by Informa Markets in India, concluded its inaugural edition at the Sheraton Grand, Brigade Gateway, Bengaluru, following four days of high-impact training, leadership discussions, original technical research and industry engagement from October 27 to 30, 2026.

Related:PwC's CISO on AI, Robots & What's Next for Security

Founded in 1997, Black Hat is one of the world's most respected cybersecurity conference series, renowned for its independent, vendor-neutral research and technical depth. Its debut in India marks a significant milestone for the country's cybersecurity ecosystem, bringing a globally recognised platform and its international community of researchers and practitioners to one of the world's fastest-growing digital economies.

The inaugural edition brought together a diverse mix of technical training, strategic discussions, cutting-edge research and hands-on engagement, with each day offering a distinct experience for the cybersecurity community.

Day 1 opened with specialised Trainings, featuring 13 hands-on courses delivered by 19 trainers and covering subjects ranging from offensive security fundamentals to securing emerging AI systems.

Day 2 was dedicated to Summit Day, bringing together 70+ speakers and 200+ CXOs, CISOs and VIP buyers across two tracks, Frontier AI Security and CISO Strategy. Discussions examined the changing cybersecurity landscape, enterprise risk and the growing strategic responsibilities of security leaders.

Days 3 and 4 featured the Briefings and Business Hall, with 35 researchers and practitioners presenting original work on emerging threats, software and cloud security, AI and digital identity, while welcoming more than 2,000 Business Hall delegates. A major attraction was Black Hat Arsenal, where 44 presenters demonstrated open-source security tools across 32 workstations and four hands-on labs, enabling attendees to engage directly with developers and explore practical security applications.

Related:NIST: Solo Shouldn't Mean Less Secure

The event brought together prominent government representatives, industry leaders and international cybersecurity experts. Summit Day was inaugurated in the presence of Lt. Gen. Rajesh Pant and Lt. Gen. M.U. Nair, both former National Cyber Security Coordinators of India; Dr. Sanjay Bahl, Director General, Indian Computer Emergency Response Team (CERT-In) and Controller of Certifying Authorities (CCA); and Brijesh Singh, IPS, Principal Secretary, Government of Maharashtra.

Dr. Bahl's keynote examined the implications of emerging AI technologies for India's digital ecosystem and the importance of strengthening cyber resilience. Nathan Hamiel, Senior Director of Research at Kudelski Security, delivered the Briefings keynote, exploring how AI is transforming cybersecurity and why human expertise remains indispensable. Shri Rajesh Kumar, IPS, Chief Executive Officer, Indian Cybercrime Co-ordination Centre (I4C), Ministry of Home Affairs, Government of India, delivered a special address on transnational cybercrime.

Related:UK Wades Into Tech Sovereignty Amid AI Policy Changes

Senior representatives from NPCI, the Reserve Bank of India, NCIIPC, PhonePe, CRED, Razorpay, Groww, Reliance, Adani Enterprises, HCLTech, EY, Deloitte, KPMG, Microsoft, Mandiant, Akamai, Zscaler and Zoho participated alongside independent and academic researchers.

Across the programme, discussions consistently highlighted the growing importance of securing and governing AI, the increasing sophistication of cyberattacks, software supply-chain vulnerabilities, digital payment fraud and deepfakes. Protecting India's Digital Public Infrastructure, preparing for post-quantum technologies and translating technical risks into business priorities also emerged as important themes.

India's rapid digital expansion is creating unprecedented opportunities, alongside an increasingly complex cybersecurity landscape. The average organisational cost of a data breach in India reached ₹25.5 crore in 2026, nearly 16% higher year on year, underlining the growing economic implications of cyber risk.

Commenting on the significance of Black Hat India's successful maiden edition, Mr. Yogesh Mudras, Managing Director, Informa Markets in India, said, "India’s rapid digital transformation has unlocked extraordinary opportunities, while also bringing the need to secure an increasingly connected economy into sharper focus. In the last financial year, UPI processed over 24,000 crore transactions worth more than ₹314 lakh crore, while CERT-In reported over 29.4 lakh cybersecurity incidents in 2025, an increase of approximately 44% over the previous year. The recent spate of global cyber incidents, including critical PHP vulnerabilities, being described as ‘Black October’, is a timely reminder that digital growth must be matched by stronger security and resilience.

The successful debut of Black Hat India is a significant achievement for us and an important addition to India’s cybersecurity ecosystem. The scale of participation and quality of discussions reflect the strong appetite for a platform of this calibre. As AI reshapes the threat landscape, staying ahead of emerging risks becomes even more critical. We are proud to have brought this globally recognised platform to India and look forward to building on this strong beginning,” he added.

The inaugural edition was supported by Diamond Partners TrendAI, Upwind and EY, alongside other sponsors and exhibitors, and shaped by the Black Hat India Review Board.

Black Hat India's debut comes in a milestone year for Informa Markets in India, marking 20 years of its presence in the country. The edition is also part of Festivity of Business, the company's annual campaign, now in its seventh year. Spanning 100 days, the campaign celebrates the optimism, energy and entrepreneurial spirit driving India's growth, bringing together the country's vibrant festive season and its high season of business events.

The successful inaugural edition establishes a significant new platform for India's cybersecurity community, bringing global expertise, original research and industry leadership together at a time when securing the digital future has never been more important.
