Beyond benchmark evaluations, we qualitatively examine the workflows enabled by the harness layer introduced in Section 2.5, covering proactive service initiation and cross-platform task execution.

We present two representative proactive-service trajectories in Figure 11 and Figure 28 of Appendix A.3. Each trajectory is organized into four highlighted stages: proactive task initiation, one or more proactive execution phases, and a decision-ready result. In Figure 11, a scheduled 08:00 trigger combines the user’s morning commitments, extracted from the highlighted notification, with live weather and commute conditions. The harness first retrieves the weather, then reasons over public-transit and taxi options through map navigation, and delivers an actionable morning brief with umbrella advice, departure timing, and key reminders before the user asks. In Figure 28, the harness detects a flight-cancellation notification (highlighted in the first stage) that puts a next-day 14:00 demo at risk. It proactively searches alternative flights and high-speed trains in two execution phases, evaluates which options arrive on time, and presents a decision-ready travel recovery plan, leaving the final booking decision to the user. Both examples show the harness converting passive notifications into contextualized, executable assistance while keeping consequential actions under user control.

Figure 12: Demonstration of cross-platform task execution. In this workflow, mobile subtasks run on virtual screens of the physical device, so execution does not block the user’s own actions. Parallel multi-app search: the agent searches sushi restaurants on Dianping, Meituan, and Amap concurrently through multiple virtual screens, consolidates their ratings, and summarizes the top three options in a local report opened on the computer.

Figure 6(II) illustrates a cross-platform workflow for team-building restaurant selection. The harness decomposes the task into four device-addressed stages: Qwen-UI-Agent discovers candidate restaurants on mobile, organizes their ratings, cuisines, and reservation phones into a spreadsheet on the desktop, sends the resulting artifact to the team leader for approval, and finally saves the approved restaurants back to the mobile map application. Throughout the workflow, the shared task state carries the candidate list, the generated spreadsheet, and the approval outcome across devices, so each stage resumes from the latest context rather than restarting from scratch. We present two executed trajectories of this capability in Figure 12 and Figure 29 of Appendix A.3: a receipt-organization workflow that hands mobile gallery selections to GUI+CLI computer use for expense-report generation, and a parallel restaurant search that operates Dianping, Meituan, and Amap concurrently on multiple virtual screens; in both cases, mobile subtasks run on virtual screens without blocking the user’s own use of the device. Together, these examples demonstrate that the harness can preserve task state, artifacts, and dependencies as execution moves between mobile and computer environments.

Aggregate benchmark scores show whether an agent completes a task, but reveal less about how it executes the task, why it fails, and how its behavior changes after RL training. We therefore examine four questions mainly on the execution behavior of Qwen-UI-Agent. (1) Why do models optimized for simulated benchmarks struggle on physical devices? (2) How does Qwen-UI-Agent combine GUI, CLI, and batched actions during task execution, and what distinctive execution patterns emerge from these choices? (3) How does action RL change step-level execution behavior? (4) How does online RL reshape trajectory-level behavior in long-horizon tasks? Together, these analyses complement aggregate metrics by revealing how the agent responds to challenging environments, uses its action space, and changes after RL training.

Most models have limited direct exposure to physical-device interaction during training. Frontier models such as Qwen 3.7 Plus perform strongly across a broad range of benchmarks, yet their results on MobileWorld-Real suggest that reliable execution on physical devices remains challenging. This raises a more specific behavioral question: when models have limited real-device interaction during training, which execution patterns emerge, and how do these patterns contribute to task failure? To investigate this question, we review every failed Qwen 3.7 Plus trajectory on MobileWorld-Real and AndroidDaily to identify the primary failure modes.

Table 10: Failure-pattern distribution over all failed Qwen 3.7 Plus trajectories on real devices.

Figure 13: Representative real-device failure patterns of Qwen 3.7 Plus. Execution capability limitations: (a) exploration failure, (b) erroneous action loops, (c) lost execution state. Real-world scenario challenges: (d) UI misreading, (e) pop-up interference, (f) physical widget control. Speech bubbles quote the model’s abridged thinking, with the critical fallacy in purple.

As shown in Table 10, we organize the failures along two dimensions, both rooted in the scarcity of real interaction experience during training: execution capability limitations (40.3%), where simulated environments provide little incentive to learn exploration, error recovery, and state tracking, and real-world scenario challenges (52.0%), triggered by interface phenomena that sanitized simulators rarely contain. As illustrated in Figure 13, we pair two key frames with the model’s abridged thinking for one representative case of each pattern, and analyze each pattern in detail below.

Qwen 3.7 Plus’ failures in this dimension (40.3%) reflect limitations in three execution capabilities: locating less commonly used or deeply nested app functions, recovering from unexpected feedback or interface states, and maintaining task state over long trajectories.

Failures in this dimension (52.0%) involve interface conditions that are common on physical devices but may be less frequently represented during training: ambiguous page semantics, unexpected interface disruptions, and interactive widgets requiring fine-grained manipulation.

The findings above motivate the real-device training and evaluation infrastructure described in Section 2.2.2 and help explain Qwen-UI-Agent’s strong performance on real-world mobile devices.

Qwen-UI-Agent executes computer-use tasks through trajectories that interleave GUI and CLI actions, while selectively emitting multiple actions together in batched form. This hybrid execution process raises two questions: how does the model divide operations between GUI and CLI, and when does it choose to batch multiple actions? We analyze trajectories generated by Qwen-UI-Agent on OSWorld-Verified and OSWorld-v2 and present the resulting behavioral patterns below.

Table 11: GUI+CLI usage and batched-execution statistics on OSWorld-Verified and OSWorld-v2. Panel (a) reports CLI and batched-action usage at the action and task levels. Panel (b) reports the composition of batched outputs. Mean batch size counts the number of primitive actions in a batch. Differences are computed as OSWorld-v2 minus OSWorld-Verified. Differences are reported in percentage points (pp).

Figure 14: Representative GUI interaction patterns. The panels show (a) filling structured spreadsheet content, (b) finding content with in-page search, (c) visual navigation through scrolling, (d) use of a native media-timeline feature, (e) continuous spatial interaction, and (f) zooming in for fine-grained inspection. Each panel pairs the rendered action with its resulting application state.

Table 11 quantifies CLI and batched execution at the action and task levels. GUI interaction and CLI operations both serve as primary execution channels in the computer-use trajectories produced by Qwen-UI-Agent. On OSWorld-Verified, CLI operations constitute 40.7% of all actions and appear in 92.0% of tasks. These proportions increase to 55.1% and 98.2% on OSWorld-v2, respectively. The near-balanced distribution of GUI and CLI actions suggests that Qwen-UI-Agent makes flexible and complementary use of both executions. The higher CLI share on OSWorld-v2 partly reflects its task distribution, which contains more workflows that naturally favor programmatic execution, such as merging files, processing structured data, and performing batch file operations.Batched actions account for 39.6% and 41.6% of all actions on OSWorld-Verified and OSWorld-v2, and appear in 62.1% and 88.9% of tasks, respectively, indicating that batched execution is used extensively across both benchmarks. With an average of 3.1 primitive actions per batch, this corresponds to significantly fewer observation–reasoning–execution cycles than issuing each primitive action in a separate model turn, substantially improving execution efficiency. GUI-only batches remain the dominant form, while CLI-only and mixed GUI+CLI batches show that batched execution also supports programmatic operations and coordination between the two execution channels.

Figure 15: Representative CLI interaction patterns. The model uses CLI tools to (a) retrieve relevant documents from a mixed corpus, (b) parse machine-readable artifact structure, (c) compress many visual candidates into one labeled comparison, (d) transform an artifact programmatically, (e) execute a repeated computation and materialize its outputs, and (f) verify exact output and process postconditions. Each panel juxtaposes the application context with a command excerpt and its task-specific purpose.

Beyond aggregate usage statistics, we examine recurring patterns in the trajectories to understand how Qwen-UI-Agent uses GUI interaction, CLI execution, and batched actions in practice. The examples below identify typical scenarios for each capability and illustrate how they complement one another during task execution, although they represent empirical tendencies rather than fixed capability boundaries.

Figure 17: Representative trajectory of tightly coupled GUI–CLI collaboration. The task requires Qwen-UI-Agent to achieve a score of at least 100 in a browser-based dinosaur game without using DevTools or CDP. GUI observations provide evidence for measuring jump dynamics, diagnosing failed control policies, and recognizing changes between day and night modes, while CLI code iteratively implements stateful key control and adaptive obstacle detection.

One particularly interesting trajectory asks Qwen-UI-Agent to play a browser-based dinosaur game and achieve a score of at least 100 without using browser developer tools or the Chrome DevTools Protocol (CDP). Issuing every jump as a separate model action is poorly suited to this task because obstacles continue moving during screenshot acquisition and model inference. Qwen-UI-Agent instead uses CLI to construct and iteratively refine a local controller that operates through rendered screenshots and standard keyboard events. The controller does not access the DOM or internal game state, leaving the visible GUI as its source of task state and the keyboard as its action interface.

The trajectory reveals a tightly coupled pattern of GUI–CLI composition. Successive GUI observations provide the evidence needed to measure jump dynamics, calibrate obstacle-trigger distances, and diagnose errors in jump timing and duration. CLI code converts these observations into explicit perception and control procedures. When repeated trials expose the limitations of stateless, fixed-duration taps, the model introduces explicit action state and holds the jump key until the obstacle has passed. A further challenge arises when the game switches from day to night mode. The resulting contrast inversion invalidates the initial assumption that obstacles correspond to dark pixels. After identifying this change from the rendered frames, Qwen-UI-Agent replaces fixed color thresholding with background-relative segmentation and uses obstacle geometry to handle both ground-level cacti and airborne birds.

The resulting controller survives for 60 seconds while executing 50 jumps and reaches a visible last-run score of 156. This case shows that GUI and CLI provide different but interdependent functions within a single trajectory. The GUI supplies perceptual evidence and execution feedback, while CLI execution turns that evidence into repeatable perception and control procedures that do not require model inference at every game step. By repeatedly moving from GUI observation to CLI-based revision and back to GUI verification, Qwen-UI-Agent constructs an effective controller without privileged access to the application.

Table 12: Performance on five error-pattern-specific test sets before and after action RL training.

Figure 18: Action RL case study. The SFT model navigates to the account page and repeatedly clicks the share icon before navigating back, becoming trapped in an ineffective local loop. In contrast, the model after action RL recognizes that it is on the wrong page, switches to exploring Mastodon’s web interface, locates the correct entry point, and successfully configures the target invite link with the required settings.

Overall Performance and Behavioral Shift. We further conduct a comparative analysis of the model behaviors before and after action RL training. The primary effect of action RL is not merely an improvement in aggregate task success, but a systematic correction of erroneous action decisions at a fine-grained, step-level granularity. Beyond improving the overall task SR by more than 7%, action RL reduces the number of reasoning tokens by 21.3% while increasing the average number of interaction steps by 8.4%. These changes suggest that the updated policy performs less redundant reasoning and more frequently translates its decisions into environment-grounded actions. More importantly, the performance gains are accompanied by a clear shift in action patterns, characterized by the systematic correction of recurring action errors, which we examine in detail below.

Correction of Recurring Error Patterns. As shown in Table 12, action RL consistently improves performance across all five error-pattern-specific test sets. On grounding tasks that require selecting the correct target among visually or semantically similar candidates, action RL improves SR by 6.3%. It also improves SR on Sorting and Ranking, Quantity and Multi-Target Completeness, and Premature Completion tasks by 3.8%, 4.4%, and 5.2%, respectively. The effect of action RL is particularly evident in correcting short-range action loops. On tasks involving repeated operations over multiple targets or navigation through long scrollable interfaces, action RL improves SR by 9.5%. This gain indicates a stronger ability to recognize when recent actions have failed to produce task-relevant state changes and to switch to an alternative action. Figure 18 presents a representative example in which the agent is asked to generate an invitation link in Mastodon. The model before action RL repeatedly interacts with the profile-sharing icon and eventually reaches the step limit. In contrast, the model after action RL recognizes that it is on the wrong page, explores an alternative path through Mastodon’s web interface, and reaches the correct interface for configuring the invitation link. This example highlights the central role of step-level action RL: when a short sequence of actions fails to make meaningful progress, the automatically constructed loop-breaking examples teach the policy to revise its immediate action choice rather than persist with an ineffective pattern.

Table 13: Performance comparison between frequent and long-tail actions.

Figure 19: Online RL elicits verification and self-correction. On the same task, the online RL policy (bottom) succeeds where the SFT policy (top) fails, because it reopens the application to inspect the actual result and corrects the problem it finds before finishing. The SFT policy trusts a superficial script check that only counts the chart, never looks at the rendered output, and ships an empty chart with no data series, whereas the RL policy reopens the file to verify, finds that a leftover application instance would overwrite the chart, removes it so the chart persists, and confirms that the full clustered chart is actually rendered before terminating.

Improved Handling of Long-tail Actions. GUI action trajectories exhibit a highly imbalanced distribution. Frequent actions such as click, drag, and type account for 80.1% of primitive actions, whereas long-tail actions such as ask_user, and long_press account for only 19.9%. Despite their low frequency, these actions are often critical for reaching specific application states or invoking specialized controls. Pass-N rollouts show that their reward is 16.8% lower than that of frequent actions, reflecting their limited coverage in the training data. Action RL therefore increases the proportion of long-tail action examples to about 40% in the training data, improving their reward by 6.4%. These results demonstrate that action RL can selectively strengthen low-frequency but high-impact action decisions.

Figure 20: Online RL elicits emergent cross-modal action. On the same task, the online RL policy (bottom) succeeds where the SFT policy (top) fails, because it grounds its decisions in what it visually observes and verifies the result before finishing. The SFT policy reads the receipt from OCR text alone, mistakes a Cash Out for income, and declares success without checking, whereas the RL policy opens the receipt image to visually read it, records it correctly as an expense, and reads the spreadsheet back to verify before terminating.

Figure 21: Online RL improves long-horizon search and verification. Without online RL, the model follows incorrect entity associations and reaches the unsupported answer Guangzhou. With online RL, it identifies the key chain of Hai Yan, Nirvana in Fire, Huang Weide, and Chengdu.

By carefully comparing the trajectories generated before and after online RL across different domains, we identify the emergence of three desirable behavioral patterns.

Before online RL, the policy often terminates immediately after its final state-changing action, assuming that the task has been completed without verifying the intended effect. After online RL, the policy more frequently inspects the resulting state before termination and recovers when verification reveals incomplete execution. Figure 19 illustrates a representative example. Before online RL, the policy performs a sequence of Bash operations and declares completion without checking their effects. After online RL, the policy initially follows the same Bash-based path but adds a verification step, discovers that part of the intended output was not generated, switches to an alternative approach, and ultimately completes the task successfully. Aggregate results on OSWorld show the same trend: the fraction of trajectories containing at least one verification action increases by 14.7%, accompanied by an 11.2% decrease in the false-stop rate—the fraction of self-declared successful episodes that fail the final verifier.

After online RL, the policy shifts from a Bash-leaning strategy to a more balanced use of Bash and GUI actions: the share of GUI actions increases by 6%, while the fraction of trajectories employing both GUI and Bash increases by 10.6 %. Notably, despite the absence of an explicit objective for modality coordination, a useful pattern of cross-modal collaboration emerges: the policy uses Bash to execute state changes efficiently and the GUI to inspect their effects. Consistent with this pattern, the fraction of trajectories exhibiting an execution–verification transition—a state-changing Bash action followed by a read-only GUI inspection—increases from 40.2% to 52.4%. Figure 20 illustrates a representative example. Before online RL, the policy relies only on Bash to execute the task and, without visual information, fails to distinguish income from expenses, introducing errors into the result. After online RL, the policy executes efficiently with Bash and then opens individual receipts in the GUI for visual inspection, allowing it to distinguish the relevant entries and complete the task correctly. This cross-modal loop combines efficient execution with reliable feedback, with Bash serving as the hands and the GUI as the eyes.

Long-horizon tasks often impose additional constraints on the primary goal, including quantities, formats, ranges, and exclusions. Comparing behavior before and after online RL, we find that the policy becomes more reliable at preserving constraints introduced earlier in the instruction throughout execution. Across the two benchmarks, the fraction of tasks satisfying all instruction constraints increases by 8.6% on OSWorld and by 7.5% on BrowseComp-ZH. Figure 21 illustrates a representative BrowseComp-ZH example involving four constraints. In this case, the policy shifts from satisfying only one constraint and reaching an incorrect conclusion to retaining and jointly applying all four, leading to the correct answer.

Recent work on GUI agents across mobile-use, browser-use, and computer-use increasingly favors native multimodal policies over prompt-only orchestration, with stronger use of self-evolving data engines, online reinforcement learning, and structured memory (Seed, 2025; Zhou et al., 2025a; Gao et al., 2026; He et al., 2024; Wang et al., 2025b; Huang et al., 2026). The field is also moving from benchmark-only optimization toward realistic deployment concerns in terms of real-device execution, privacy, operational reliability, safety, and latency (Cao et al., 2026; Qin et al., 2025; Zhou et al., 2025a; Huang et al., 2026).

Mobile Use. Recent mobile agent works increasingly move beyond screenshot-grounded action prediction toward more capable systems with memory, reflection, RL, and agentic harness. Mobile-Agent-v3.5 (Xu et al., 2026) and UI-Venus-1.5 (Gao et al., 2026) scale unified GUI models across diverse platforms and tasks. Step-GUI (Yan et al., 2025) and UI-TARS-2 (Wang et al., 2025a) investigate data flywheels and multi-turn RL for improving agent capability through iterative interaction. MAI-UI (Zhou et al., 2025a) explores hybrid GUI-MCP operations and device–cloud collaboration. Xiaomi-GUI-0 (Cao et al., 2026) studies real-device closed-loop training that captures state dynamics. Existing mobile agent evaluations mainly focus on three aspects: grounding capability (e.g., ScreenSpot-Pro (Li et al., 2025a)), interactive task completion in controlled environments (e.g., AndroidWorld (Rawles et al., 2024)), and more realistic daily-use scenarios such as MobileWorld (Kong et al., 2026), AndroidDaily (Sui et al., 2026), and RealMobile (Cao et al., 2026).

Browser Use. Compared with mobile agents, browser-use agents can additionally leverage structured information such as DOM trees, accessibility trees, and browser APIs. Early research such as WebVoyager (He et al., 2024) and SeeAct (Zheng et al., 2024) demonstrate the capability of multimodal LLMs to perform end-to-end web navigation by combining visual understanding with browser actions. Recent projects, such as OpenAI Operator (OpenAI, 2025), Google Project Mariner (Google, 2025), and UI-TARS (Seed, 2025), further extend web agents toward autonomous interaction with complex websites and diverse digital environments. Some recent studies like WebWorld (Xiao et al., 2026) and WebEvolver (Fang et al., 2025) also explore world-model-based approaches for web-agent. Web-agent evaluation has evolved from short-horizon webpage navigation toward realistic long-horizon workflows involving multiple websites and complex objectives (Zhou et al., 2024; Xue et al., 2025; Jang et al., 2026).

Computer Use. Computer-use agents (CUA) aim to enable general interaction with desktop environments, covering diverse software and productivity applications. Compared with mobile and web agents, CUAs face a significantly larger action space, more diverse interaction states, and longer horizon, making reliable grounding and execution verification critical. Recent efforts including UI-TARS (Qin et al., 2025), DART-GUI (Li et al., 2025b), OpenCUA (Wang et al., 2025b), UltraCUA (Yang et al., 2025b), EvoCUA (Huang et al., 2026) investigate scalable GUI foundation models by leveraging multimodal pretraining, large-scale interaction data, and agentic reasoning. Recent CUA benchmarks increasingly emphasize robustness, scalability, and real-world deployment challenges. OSWorld-Verified improves evaluation reliability through stricter verification protocols and reduced ambiguity in success assessment (Xie et al., 2024). OSWorld-v2 further scales evaluation toward more complex, long-horizon workflows and diverse real-world computer tasks (Yuan et al., 2026).

We presented Qwen-UI-Agent, a real-world centric foundation GUI agent. Guided by a vision of agents that operate on real devices, maintain workflows across platforms, combine GUI interaction with CLI execution, and proactively initiate useful services, Qwen-UI-Agent integrates scalable sandbox environments with a robust real-device mobile runtime, a unified GUI+CLI action space, an agent-driven data flywheel, a training framework combining SFT, action RL, and online RL, and a harness layer for proactive service initiation and cross-platform task execution. Empirically, Qwen-UI-Agent achieves 82.1% on MobileWorld, 92.2% on MobileWorld-Real, and 97.5% on AndroidDaily. It also reaches 79.5% on OSWorld-Verified, and 40.0% on OSWorld-v2. Qwen-UI-Agent further obtains leading or competitive performance across browser use, DeepSearch, and GUI-grounding, while preserving strong general reasoning and agentic capabilities. Together, these results support a systems view of GUI agent development where models, environments, data flywheel, training, and harness are co-designed around real-world usage.

First, we evaluate real-device mobile trajectories using AutoJudge rather than deterministic verifiers or manual expert examination. Deterministic state-based verification is generally infeasible on physical phones because of the restrict access to the internal states of third-party applications. Meanwhile, manually adjudicating trajectories from Qwen-UI-Agent and ten baselines would require substantial expert effort and introduce inter-annotator variation. To ensure a consistent comparison, we apply the same AutoJudge protocol to all systems. On 666 examples independently examined by experts, AutoJudge achieves 92.8% exact-match accuracy. Nevertheless, its remaining errors may introduce minor uncertainty into the reported real-device results. Second, CUA and DeepSearch training at the 35B-A3B scale was still in progress when this technical report was released, so the corresponding results are not included. We plan to update the arXiv version once these results become available. Third, beyond the real-device environments described in this report, higher-fidelity synthetic environments that more closely reproduce real applications provide another path to narrowing the simulation-to-real gap. We have constructed such environments, but training with them was not yet incorporated into the reported model, so this component is not presented in the current report. We plan to open-source the environment synthesis methodology in future work. Fourth, our attempts to fully automate GUI capability development showed that current foundation models cannot yet manage the entire process reliably. Our pipeline is therefore agent-driven rather than fully autonomous and still requires considerable human oversight and intervention to monitor progress and correct errors.

We discuss several directions that we believe warrant further investigation as GUI agents move toward reliable and practical real-world use.

Zhou et al. (2024) Shuyan Zhou, Frank F. Xu, Hao Zhu, Xuhui Zhou, Robert Lo, Abishek Sridhar, Xianyi Cheng, Tianyue Ou, Yonatan Bisk, Daniel Fried, Uri Alon, and Graham Neubig. Webarena: A realistic web environment for building autonomous agents. In The Twelfth International Conference on Learning Representations, 2024. URL https://arxiv.org/abs/2307.13854.

Unlike sandbox benchmarks, real-device tasks generally cannot rely on deterministic, state-based verifiers. Third-party Apps do not expose their internal states, while task outcomes may depend on changing online content, account status, or interactions across multiple Apps. Manually reviewing every trajectory is also difficult to scale and can introduce variation between annotators. We therefore validate AutoJudge against independent human annotations before using it for large-scale real-device evaluation.

Table 14: Agreement between AutoJudge and independent human annotations. Rows denote AutoJudge decisions and columns denote human annotations. pass corresponds to the combined perfect/fair_enough category. Agreement is computed after excluding human annotations marked as unclear.

Table 14 reports the comparison. AutoJudge agrees with the human annotations on 618 of the 666 trajectories with a conclusive human label, corresponding to an overall agreement of 92.8%. Agreement reaches 96.2% for successful trajectories. Model failures and environment errors are more difficult to distinguish, since similar observations can result from either an incorrect Agent action or a problem with the live execution environment.

We further ask expert reviewers to examine the cases in which AutoJudge and the initial human annotation disagree. Expert adjudication more often supports the AutoJudge decision, indicating that the agreement reported above is a conservative estimate of its accuracy. Based on this validation, we use AutoJudge as the primary evaluator for our real-device experiments. It provides a more accurate and scalable evaluation protocol while separately identifying model failures and errors caused by the live environment.

Figures 22–24 show representative examples of the three AutoJudge outcomes. Each example includes the original Chinese task and its English translation, selected trajectory steps with actions rendered on the screenshots, and the evidence used for the final decision. These examples illustrate how AutoJudge distinguishes successful execution and model failures from errors caused by the live execution environment.

Figure 22: Representative AutoJudge pass decisions. The trajectory evidence shows that the Agent successfully completes the requested task.

Figure 23: Representative AutoJudge model-failure decisions. The Agent follows an incorrect or incomplete execution path and does not satisfy the task requirements.

Figure 24: Representative AutoJudge environment-error decisions. The trajectories are interrupted by live-App or execution-environment issues rather than by the Agent’s actions.

Figure 25: Demonstration of long-horizon real-device GUI execution. The trajectory is rendered as key frames with the executed action annotated beneath each frame. In this 23-step shopping task, the agent decomposes the instruction into sequential sub-goals: it first empties the Taobao shopping cart, then selects a smart speaker, a smart plug, and a smart bulb from the Xiaomi flagship store within a 1,000-yuan budget, maintaining the budget constraint and the item checklist throughout.

Figure 26: Demonstration of hybrid GUI+CLI execution in computer-use tasks. In this example, the agent uses CLI tools to inspect spreadsheet contents and interleaves GUI actions, including batched hotkey sequences and Paste Special, to transfer the formatted data into a Writer document.

Figure 27: Demonstration of DeepSearch invoked on demand during GUI execution. Qwen-UI-Agent first locates a Douyin video on evidence-based weight loss and extracts its main claims through GUI actions, then calls DeepSearch mid-trajectory to verify these claims against research papers and authoritative health sources, supplying external evidence that the interface itself does not provide. The verification conclusions directly determine the subsequent GUI actions, and the agent returns to Douyin to post an evidence-grounded comment, closing an observe–research–decide–act loop.

Figure 28: Demonstration of proactive service based on mobile notifications. The trajectory is organized into highlighted stages: proactive task initiation from a detected notification, proactive execution phases, and a decision-ready result, with the executed action annotated beneath each key frame. A scheduled morning brief: at 08:00 the harness combines the user’s morning commitments with live weather and commute conditions, compares public-transit and taxi options before the user asks, and delivers an actionable brief with umbrella advice, departure timing, and key reminders.

Figure 29: Demonstrations of cross-platform task execution. In this workflow, mobile subtasks run on virtual screens of the physical device, so execution does not block the user’s own actions. Receipt organization across devices: the agent locates receipt images in the photo gallery on a mobile virtual screen, transfers them to the designated PC directory, and uses GUI+CLI computer use to consolidate the receipt details into a summary expense spreadsheet.

Anyone Can Copy Your Crypto App. Can They Copy Your Business?

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Business Developement Manager with a demonstrated history of working in the public relations and communication.

Business Developement Manager with a demonstrated history of working in the public relations and communication.

Business Developement Manager with a demonstrated history of working in the public relations and communication.

For much of his career, Aleksei Voiutskii has been working on a problem that technology founders often underestimate: how to turn a product into a viable business.

He founded NODES as a marketing consultancy, later expanded its work into technology solutions, and now evaluates emerging companies from another perspective – as a private investor and business angel. Through NODES’ Startup Accelerator and Crypto Advisory practices, he also helps technology founders navigate the journey from product development to market entry and scale.

That combination of experience in marketing, user acquisition, technology, and investing gives Voiutskii a particular perspective on one of the questions facing Web3 today: what happens to a startup when the technology behind its product becomes easy to replicate?

“When I evaluate a startup, the number of impressive features it has is not what interests me most,” Voiutskii says. “I want to understand why customers would choose this particular business, trust it with their money, and keep using its product even if a competitor offered almost identical technology.”

For Web3, that question is becoming harder to ignore.

AI-powered development tools are lowering the barrier to entry. Open APIs provide access to sophisticated trading infrastructure. Features that once required substantial engineering resources can now be reproduced in a fraction of the time.

But as building a crypto application becomes easier, convincing users to trust it becomes a much more difficult proposition.

Security has been a fundamental concern in crypto since the industry's earliest days. Over the years, companies have significantly improved the technologies used to protect digital assets. But as financial infrastructure becomes more sophisticated, risk management grows more complicated.

Today, the security of a trading platform depends on far more than the underlying blockchain or its smart contracts. Third-party providers, internal systems, authentication mechanisms, and operational procedures all contribute to the platform's overall resilience.

Recent incidents involving major crypto platforms illustrate just how much this broader infrastructure matters.

On October 29, Polymarket reported a security incident involving a third-party technology provider. The incident did not involve a vulnerability in Polymarket’s smart contracts, which continued to function as intended. As a precaution, the company temporarily suspended trading, addressed the issue, and restored platform operations. Polymarket also said it would fully compensate affected users for their losses.

On October 30, Binance published preliminary findings from a separate security investigation. The exchange outlined measures taken to contain the incident, including temporarily suspending deposits and withdrawals for additional checks and freezing some affected funds.

Binance also stated that it had found no evidence that wallet private keys had been compromised and said it intended to cover users' losses.

The incidents differed in both their causes and circumstances. But they illustrate a common reality of running a financial platform: security is not simply about preventing something from going wrong. It is also about responding effectively when something does go wrong, protecting customers, and keeping the business operational.

What is changing is the competitive environment in which these responsibilities must be fulfilled.

Users now have access to a growing number of products built on increasingly similar technological foundations. As functionality becomes easier to reproduce, reliability, security, and user experience become more important ways for companies to differentiate themselves.

And trust is not built through security architecture alone. It also depends on whether a company consistently delivers on its promises to customers.

A different challenge is emerging for the next generation of Web3 founders.

The question is no longer simply how to build technology that competitors do not have.

It is how to build a product that customers will continue choosing once similar technology becomes widely available.

These are conventional business questions. Yet for an industry whose growth has long been driven by technological breakthroughs, they are taking on renewed significance.

“Technology still matters, but access to it no longer makes a company exceptional on its own,” Voiutskii argues. “If several startups can build essentially the same product, I want to know which one understands its customers better, can deliver a more reliable service, and has a realistic strategy for growth.”

Some younger Web3 companies are already approaching product development with this logic in mind.

Rather than building every technological component from scratch, they are combining existing infrastructure around specific customer needs.

One example is Sparkling, a trading application that comes with an agent and brings together established market infrastructure and AI to simplify access to different financial instruments. AI prepares every trade, but the final tap on the Confirm button always belongs to the user.

Its approach provides a useful way to examine two distinct challenges: building a better trading tool and building a sustainable business around it.

Crypto traders have access to an expanding range of financial markets. But participating in them often means moving between separate applications, interfaces, and workflows.

Sparkling is attempting to reduce that fragmentation.

The self-custodial trading application provides access to perpetual futures through Hyperliquid and Lighter, prediction markets through Polymarket, tokenized US equities through xStocks, and token swaps using infrastructure that includes Bebop.

The product is available through two different interfaces.

Its Telegram application allows users to access trading tools directly within the messaging platform.

The web application, launched on October 14, offers a more comprehensive trading terminal, including an order book and additional market data for users who prefer to analyze markets and manage positions themselves.

Both versions support manual trading as well as AI-assisted workflows.

One of the application's central features is a mechanism called Pre-Executed Flow.

Instead of manually entering every parameter of a trade, users can describe what they want to do in natural language.

The AI agent then prepares an order, including the relevant parameters such as price, position size, execution route, leverage, stop loss and take profit.

The user reviews the proposed transaction and can modify its parameters or cancel it altogether.

The trade is not executed until the user explicitly confirms it.

This design addresses a broader tension facing AI-powered financial products.

According to an MEXC study published in October 2026, 84.8% of 3,708 surveyed crypto traders had used AI for market analysis or trading. Yet only 20% had experimented with allowing AI to execute trades independently, without requiring confirmation for each transaction.

Users may welcome AI assistance when navigating complex markets. That does not necessarily mean they are willing to surrender control over their money.

Sparkling's approach to asset control is based on a self-custodial model.

Its integrated wallet uses infrastructure provided by Turnkey, which has publicly acknowledged the integration.

According to Sparkling's published description of its architecture, the application does not hold customer funds or have access to users' private keys. Its AI agent also cannot independently sign transactions.

The architecture is intended to reduce a specific category of risk: dependence on an intermediary that holds customer assets and controls access to them.

But self-custody is not a blanket guarantee of security.

Malicious interfaces, compromised external services, and fraudulent transaction-approval requests can still expose users to losses.

Recent security incidents reinforce a broader point: the safety of a financial product depends not only on how assets are held, but on the entire infrastructure through which transactions are initiated, authorized, and executed.

Seen in that context, preventing the AI agent from independently executing transactions is an architectural choice designed to manage risk.

As similar safeguards become more common, however, their competitive value will depend increasingly on how reliably they work in practice.

A security feature can be replicated. A consistent record of protecting customers is considerably harder to reproduce.

Some of the less visible challenges facing crypto startups begin where the product demo ends.

Sparkling relies on infrastructure provided by independent companies, including Turnkey, Polymarket, Bebop, xStocks, Hyperliquid, and Lighter.

Several of these integrations have been publicly acknowledged by the respective providers.

Sparkling participates in the Polymarket Builders Program as a verified builder. Turnkey, Bebop, and xStocks have acknowledged their integrations with the application. Lighter has separately announced that its trading infrastructure is accessible through Sparkling.

These acknowledgments establish the existence of the integrations. They should not be confused with a broader endorsement of Sparkling or a commercial partnership beyond what each provider has publicly confirmed.

But even when the underlying infrastructure is available, turning multiple integrations into one coherent product involves far more than connecting a few APIs.

Trading venues have different mechanisms for authentication, order placement, fee calculation, collateral management, and settlement.

A company bringing these systems together must account for those differences while presenting users with an experience that feels consistent and understandable.

“Connecting an API is one thing. Making it part of a reliable financial product is something else entirely,” Kotlov says. “There is a tremendous amount of engineering, organizational, and operational work between gaining access to a market and building a service people genuinely find easy to use.”

This is where the distinction between technology and business becomes particularly clear.

APIs can provide access to markets. They cannot, by themselves, guarantee that users will understand an interface, complete transactions successfully, receive support when something fails, or return to the product the following week.

Those outcomes depend on the business operating behind the technology.

And as more startups gain access to the same infrastructure, the ability to deliver those outcomes consistently may become a more durable advantage than the integrations themselves.

The implications extend well beyond any single trading application.

As AI reduces development costs and more companies build on the same infrastructure, three considerations are likely to carry increasing weight in Web3 competition.

Technology must solve a real customer problem.

Access to sophisticated tools does not automatically constitute a competitive advantage. What matters is what users can actually accomplish with those tools – and whether the product makes that process meaningfully better.

Security and usability must reinforce each other.

Even a technically robust system can drive customers away if it is too difficult to use. Conversely, a convenient interface that obscures financial risks can destroy trust just as quickly.

The challenge is to make risk visible and understandable without turning every transaction into an obstacle course.

Reliability must extend beyond trade execution.

Transparent fees, consistent transaction execution, responsive customer support, and effective incident management are not secondary features. They are essential parts of a financial service.

For Voiutskii, these considerations are also becoming more important when evaluating potential investments.

“For years, startups competed for the attention of investors and users by demonstrating technologies people had never seen before,” he says. “Now, increasingly, they have to compete like any other business: provide a reliable service, understand their customers, solve real problems, and keep improving the user experience. Technology alone is no longer enough to succeed.”

The consequences of that shift could be substantial.

AI can make it easier to build a competing application. Open infrastructure can make it faster to reproduce its features.

Neither automatically produces customer trust.

The next competitive advantage in Web3 may have less to do with building something nobody else can build – and much more to do with delivering consistent value when everyone has access to the same technology.

Lava Security’s Yakir Kadkoda on the AI Data Center Boom: Growth, Security, and the Risks in Between

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Tech analyst and reporter covering startups, public tech companies, and executives. Associate Editor at The San Francisco Tribune.

Tech analyst and reporter covering startups, public tech companies, and executives. Associate Editor at The San Francisco Tribune.

Tech analyst and reporter covering startups, public tech companies, and executives. Associate Editor at The San Francisco Tribune.

The rapid expansion of artificial intelligence is driving demand for computing power, placing data centers at the center of a growing debate in the United States. While new facilities promise greater computing capacity, concerns over electricity costs, water consumption, and environmental impact have prompted calls for tighter oversight, including proposals to pause new construction.

Beyond these concerns lies another challenge: securing the infrastructure that powers AI. As companies increasingly rent GPU capacity from specialized cloud providers known as neoclouds, questions arise about hardware security, shared infrastructure, and the isolation of customer workloads.

Lava Security is a cybersecurity company focused on protecting neoclouds and AI data centers. Its platform provides visibility across computing, networking, storage, and hardware management systems, helping operators identify vulnerabilities, verify firmware integrity, and detect security gaps between tenants.

We spoke with Yakir Kadkoda, Co-Founder and CTO of Lava Security, who previously served as Director of Security Research at Aqua Security and currently co-leads the OWASP Top 10 for Neocloud and AI Data Center Security Risks project. He discusses the debate over data center expansion, vulnerabilities in GPU cloud infrastructure, and the responsibilities of operators, customers, and policymakers.

A: AI is not a feature anymore, it is infrastructure, like power or roads. Every hospital, bank, university and manufacturer is going to run models, and most of them cannot buy GPUs by the thousand. They rent them. More data centers means more supply, lower prices and shorter waits for everyone who is not a hyperscaler.

Three examples outside Big Tech. A hospital group running its own models on patient data: it cannot send that data to a public API, so it needs local or rented GPUs, and today it waits months or pays a premium. A university lab that queues for weeks on a shared cluster, where more capacity means faster science. A startup on a neocloud with no capex, living on rented GPUs, which feels every price hike first.

A: Startups, researchers, and smaller companies would pay the price first.

Big Tech already owns enormous compute capacity. Everyone else depends on renting it. Restricting new data centers would drive prices up, reduce availability, and concentrate even more power in the hands of the largest players.

And AI development wouldn’t stop. It would simply move to countries that continue investing in infrastructure.

A: Between March and May 2026, our research identified more than 2,100 servers publicly exposing monitoring data from over 12,000 NVIDIA GPUs, representing roughly $100 million in hardware.

Nearly 60% reported zero utilization when we observed them.

That doesn’t mean 60% of GPUs are permanently idle. Some could have been between jobs, reserved, or undergoing maintenance. It’s a snapshot of a specific exposed population, not the entire industry.

The bigger finding isn’t unused GPUs. It’s that thousands of expensive AI systems were leaking operational information to anyone on the internet.

We need better visibility, security, and utilization of existing infrastructure alongside building more capacity. These are complementary goals, not competing ones.

A: They’re right that infrastructure growth needs to be responsible. Electricity, water, environmental impact, and security are legitimate concerns.

But stopping construction is the wrong solution.

We don’t stop building hospitals because they consume electricity. We improve how they’re built and operated.

The same principle applies to AI infrastructure. Set clear requirements, enforce them, and make operators accountable.

The answer to infrastructure challenges is better infrastructure, not less infrastructure.

And there’s a national competitiveness dimension. If America slows down while other countries accelerate, the demand for AI doesn’t disappear. The infrastructure, investment, and innovation simply move elsewhere.

A: I’d focus on three things: measurable economic benefits, responsible resource consumption, and operational security.

The developer should clearly explain how much electricity and water the facility will consume, who pays for infrastructure upgrades, and how many permanent jobs it will create.

Those commitments should be measurable and enforceable.

I’d also require a credible security plan. Modern AI data centers are complex infrastructure environments with thousands of interconnected systems, including servers, management controllers, networking equipment, and industrial control systems.

Communities should welcome investment, but demand transparency and accountability.

The goal should be to build data centers that benefit both the local community and the broader economy.

A: You give up control of the infrastructure, but you don’t give up the security risk.

When you rent GPUs, you’re trusting someone else with the physical servers, firmware, networking, storage, and management systems running your workloads.

You’re also sharing infrastructure with other customers, sometimes on the same physical machines or interconnected networks.

The biggest challenge is visibility. Most customers can monitor their workloads but have almost no visibility into the infrastructure underneath them.

They cannot independently verify whether other tenants are properly isolated, whether the underlying hardware is secure, or whether previous customers left anything behind.

The fundamental problem is that customers are expected to trust infrastructure they cannot see or verify.

A: We rented GPU infrastructure just like any regular customer would.

From that environment, we discovered that security boundaries which appeared to exist at the container or virtual machine level didn’t extend to the underlying infrastructure.

In one case, we could reach internal management systems and infrastructure that should never have been accessible to a customer.

That created a potential path from a single rented machine into the provider’s broader infrastructure, putting other customers and their workloads at risk.

The most concerning part? We didn’t need sophisticated exploits. In some cases, the problem was simply how the infrastructure had been configured.

Across our research, we identified serious control-plane security vulnerabilities at different providers. We responsibly disclosed the findings, and the affected providers addressed them.

A: The provider needs to ensure the previous customer is completely gone, not just from the operating system, but from the underlying infrastructure.

That means securely sanitizing storage, removing credentials and encryption keys, clearing persistent data, and verifying the integrity of firmware and management controllers.

A simple reboot or operating system reinstall isn’t sufficient.

The challenge is economic. GPU providers make money when their hardware is running workloads, not when it’s undergoing security verification.

That creates pressure to minimize the time between customers.

But speed cannot come at the expense of isolation. One customer’s data, models, or credentials should never become accessible to the next.

And customers should be able to verify that these protections actually exist.

A: One of the biggest blind spots is the Baseboard Management Controller, or BMC.

It’s essentially a separate computer inside every physical server, with powerful capabilities to control and manage the hardware remotely, even when the main server is turned off.

Traditional cloud security and endpoint security tools generally don’t monitor this layer.

In our research, we identified more than 36,000 management interfaces exposed to the internet, many with serious authentication vulnerability.

An attacker who compromises a BMC can control the entire physical server, independently of the operating system and the security software running on it.

And if management networks aren’t properly isolated, one compromised controller can become a path into an entire fleet.

This is why securing AI infrastructure requires visibility beyond operating systems, containers, and cloud APIs.

You cannot protect infrastructure you don’t even know exists.

A: First, proof of tenant isolation. I want evidence that other customers cannot access my workloads, storage, networking, or underlying management infrastructure.

Second, proof of hardware and firmware integrity. I want to know that the physical infrastructure I’m running on hasn’t been compromised, including the GPUs, management controllers, and networking equipment.

Third, proof of secure tenant handover. I want evidence that previous customers’ data and credentials were properly removed and that mine will be too.

These are exactly the kinds of risks we’re addressing through the OWASP Top 10 for Neocloud and AI Data Center Security Risks.

And here’s what wouldn’t satisfy me: a SOC 2 certificate alone.

Compliance is important, but being compliant doesn’t automatically mean your infrastructure is secure.

You want technical evidence, not just paperwork.

A: First, accountability to the community. Operators should make clear, enforceable commitments around electricity, water, infrastructure costs, and local economic benefits.

Second, independently verified infrastructure security. Before hosting sensitive workloads, operators should demonstrate that their infrastructure is secure across the entire stack, from physical hardware and firmware to networking, virtualization, and tenant isolation.

Today, many security assessments focus on applications and operating systems while overlooking the infrastructure underneath them.

That’s a dangerous gap, particularly as AI data centers become critical infrastructure.

Our OWASP Top 10 for Neocloud and AI Data Center Security Risks provides a starting point for establishing common security expectations.

Verification should be performed independently, with meaningful evidence available to customers.

We don’t need to slow down AI infrastructure development. We need to make security and accountability part of how that infrastructure is built from day one.

Are You Making This Common Mistake With Grief?

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Become a subscribing member today. Help us continue to bring “the science of a meaningful life” to you and to millions around the globe.

Greater Good Science Center • Magazine • In Action • In Education

In recent years, the language of therapy has moved beyond the clinic and into everyday conversation, giving us new ways to name our inner lives and, at times, new ways to misread them. Nowhere is this more evident than in how we respond to grief.

In grief, we learn quickly which words help and which ones wound. “At least they lived a long life.” “They wouldn’t want you to be sad.” Most of us recognize these as unhelpful sentiments.

Not all harmful responses to grief sound like clichés, though. Sometimes they arrive dressed in compassion, wrapped in the language of awareness. A friend might say, “This seems to be activating your abandonment wound,” “Maybe this pain is really about your father,” or “Maybe this is more about your unhappiness in your marriage.” The words may be well-intentioned, even psychologically accurate, yet the effect can be deeply unsettling. Instead of feeling seen, we feel studied; instead of comforted, diagnosed.

This dynamic can feel like a kind of gaslighting because the tools and language of healing are used to explain away your emotions instead of tending to them. Unlike gaslighting in the usual sense, it isn’t malicious. It’s the experience of being told what your pain really means instead of having that pain simply met. It is a byproduct of a therapy-literate culture that often privileges interpretation over presence.

For the past several years, I’ve taught death and dying at the university level and led grief retreats. Again and again, I’ve watched grief ease, not when someone explained it, but when someone stayed with it. In what follows, I’ll look at how analysis can crowd out presence, and offer ways to stay with grief, someone else’s or your own, without rushing to interpret it.

When we’re grieving, people want to help, but few things wound more than having sorrow dissected while it’s still unfolding. Well-meaning insight can become a form of distance. A loved one may believe they’re offering understanding when they say, “This loss must be reminding you of when you lost your job,” or “You seem to struggle with change, don’t you?,” yet the grieving heart hears something different: that the pain belongs to something older and is therefore less legitimate.

Both can be true. A new loss often awakens older ones, but truth without warmth is just analysis. The body doesn’t sort its ache into past and present; it simply calls for accompaniment. In one well-known study, holding the hand of a trusted person calmed the brain’s response to threat, no words required. Seeing where a present loss touches earlier wounds can be deeply healing, eventually. But categorizing pain does not lessen the need to feel it.

We can also engage in this kind of gaslighting internally. Once we have absorbed enough psychological language, we can turn the same clinical lens on ourselves. We catch the echo of a past wound and then scold our grief for being disproportionate. “I’m overreacting,” we tell ourselves. “This is just my old stuff.” “Why can’t I just be happy?” Yet grief does not care how insightfully we label it. It still needs space to breathe. Awareness can be a form of care, but only when it does not rush ahead of the feeling itself. If it becomes a strategy to bypass feeling, our own or another’s, it defeats itself.

Many guests arrive at our retreats saying some version of “I should be over this by now.” Over the days that follow, that urgency to be finished often quiets, not through argument, but because the grief is finally given room. On the last day, they complete a ritual deep in the forest, at a fountain carved with the outline of a family. Around it, they leave rocks painted with the names of the people they’ve lost. The oldest rocks have since faded almost past recognition, the paint softening into a weathered imprint.

I’ve come to see this not as loss, but as integration: the sharp color of a fresh wound, still seeking an explanation, settling into something that no longer needs to announce itself. No one stood over the rocks interpreting them. They simply bore witness. Grief often becomes more endurable in exactly this way: when it is allowed to change shape in the space of time, care, and presence.

These suggestions come from years of sitting with grieving people, and from my own experience of loss. They are less techniques than reminders of what the moment is asking for.

Resist the urge to explain. The interpretive reflex—reaching for meaning, context, or psychological framing—is often a response to our own helplessness in the face of grief’s rawness. Notice when you’re doing it, pause, and return to the simpler question: Can I be here with this person, without needing it to mean something right now?

Let the silence be full rather than empty. Many people fill silence because it feels like abandonment. A silence in which you remain present, your body in the room, your attention undivided, can be the most companioning thing you offer. “I’m here,” said once and then honored, is worth more than 10 minutes of careful insight.

Follow their lead on meaning. If the grieving person begins to find meaning themselves, to name connections, to speak about what this loss is touching in their past, that is theirs to do. Your role is to witness it, not to initiate it. There is a world of difference between meaning that emerges from the griever and meaning that is handed to them. When you catch yourself doing it, try asking a different question: What does this need? Usually the answer is simpler than any interpretation: company, time, permission to be exactly this intense.

If you feel the pull to theorize about someone else’s grief, a few questions can help you track it in time: Am I trying to ease their pain, or my own discomfort with it? Did they ask for insight, or am I offering it uninvited? Have I acknowledged the loss itself, fully, before reaching for what it might mean?

When you’re unsure what to say, three phrases rarely go wrong: “Tell me about them.” “What do you miss most today?” “I’m here.” Each one keeps the person grieving at the center.

Grief has a way of making us distrust our own experience. We begin measuring whether we’re grieving correctly, whether we’ve stayed sad too long or not long enough, or whether the intensity means something beyond the loss itself.

Catch the self-diagnosis. This one is not easy, because the narrating voice sounds so reasonable: “This is really about my mother,” “I’m being too sensitive,” “I’m being disproportionate.” It rarely announces itself as unkind.

Notice when you’re applying the same analytical approach to yourself that you’d resist from someone else. Then let the intensity of the feeling stand without demanding it justify itself first.

Ask your grief what it needs, not what it means. Meaning can come later, if it comes at all. In the acute moment, the more honest question is practical: You might need rest, someone to call, permission to cancel a plan. Let the meaning-making wait.

Choose your witnesses. Some people simply cannot stay in the uncertainty that grief creates. Their discomfort shows up as advice, reassurance, or psychological evaluation. It doesn’t make them uncaring. It simply means they may not be the people to rely on in the rawest moments of loss. Notice who can sit in the room without needing to fix or explain, and lean on them more.

Insight has a place in grief. In time, many people do come to see how one loss is woven through the others, and that understanding can bring relief. But it tends to arrive on its own schedule, after the pain has been felt and met. Until then, what we can offer, to someone else or to ourselves, is company. The rocks at the fountain didn’t fade because anyone explained them. They faded because they were left where they were, held by time and by the people who stood beside them. Even when a wound is old, the tenderness it requires is always immediate.

Naomi Sanderovsky, Ph.D., is the founder of Luma Via retreats and trainings, and the creator of Living Grief Theory and Felt Sense Dynamics. She has taught world religions, global philosophy, and more recently death and dying at the university level for more than 20 years.

New research is exploring how our brains translate other people's actions into the experience of feeling loved.

When Lisa Kwon lost someone dear to her, she found meaning and comfort in playing Pokémon Pokopia.

Interactions among male chimpanzees involve touch, caring, vulnerability, and cooperation.

What does it actually take to put people over politics? Republican and Democrat students discover ways to resist the pull to demean or villainize people with whom they strongly disagree.

Discover how writing about our ancestors can strengthen our sense of identity, deepen gratitude, and help us find meaning in our own lives.

The Greater Good Science Center studies the psychology, sociology, and neuroscience of well-being, and teaches skills that foster a thriving, resilient, and compassionate society.

Personal Consumer Issues • Re: When is it “too late” to come back to a second career?

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

I think it depends on your skill set, attitude, network, and the need / demand for you in your industry. I returned to my 7 year second career in Defense Bus Development after 2 and 1/2 years away at age 53 then worked until I wanted to re-re retire at age 60. I did some part-time work in the defense industry during my time away.

I was a retired military flyer with a good understanding of the use of surveillance radars and had learned in industry how the defense acquisition process worked (an in demand skill set). I worked hard, left my job on good terms with my co-workers, stayed in touch with my network, and was humble when I interviewed w/ prev coworkers and when I asked my boss for my old job back.

When re entering the work force I told them the truth, I wanted to stay busier and felt I still had something to contribute to help winning new business. And we did win some new ground based radar business.

Of course there was some luck involved. And I doubled my investments working / investing the additional income from age 53 to 60 (2014-2020).

Samsung Gallery officially adds Google Photos backup, but not for all Galaxy users

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Samsung has officially launched Google Photos integration in its Gallery app with backup services, and it’s also now widely rolled out for Galaxy devices.

Samsung Gallery started rolling out support for Google Photos backup in September, but it wasn’t widely available at the time. As of this week, though, Samsung is officially announcing the feature as it is now available broadly to Galaxy users.

“Gallery Sync” in the Samsung Gallery app replaces the previous Microsoft OneDrive integration with Google Photos, letting users sign in with their Google account of choice and use the free 15GB or paid Google One storage to back up photos and videos. This allows the Samsung Gallery app to show media backed up from another Galaxy device, but it doesn’t integrate your full Google Photos library.

At this point, Google Photos backup via Samsung Gallery should be broadly available to Galaxy owners. We’re seeing Samsung’s pop-up (pictured above) about the feature on our Galaxy Z Fold 8, with the feature fully functional beyond that.

You can also access setup via Menu > Settings > “Sync photos and videos” in the Samsung Gallery app.

Samsung requires your device to be running One UI 9 (Android 17) to access this feature, though it says that support will be “expanding to additional Galaxy devices in phases.” For the time being, though, that means the feature is a bit limited for users, as the One UI 9 rollout is still in progress.

Once media has been backed up to Google Photos, Samsung Gallery also now has a “free up space” button that can delete the local copies of your photos and videos to open up storage on your phone.

A Chinese team made a big splash with a drone swarm world record

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Researchers have set a record for the largest swarm of underwater drones in history. Officially recognized by Guinness World Records, the trial saw 116 TH-618 drones deployed in a 164 ft by 65 ft pool, where they autonomously executed a series of formations and maneuvers. (For comparison, and Olympic-sized swimming pool is approximately 164 ft by 82 ft.)

The August 2026 test has strong ties to the People's Republic of China's military pipeline. It was conducted by researchers at Northwestern Polytechnical University, an institution designated by the US government as a developer of technologies for the People's Liberation Army (PLA). The university conducted the experiment in conjunction with Xi'an Tianhe Maritime Technologies, a subsidiary of defense contractor Xian Tianhe Defense Technology.

According to the South China Morning Post, the university said that developing submersible drone swarms "has always been a core challenge in the global field of underwater intelligent equipment. This time, the stable navigation of a cluster of hundreds of vehicles represents a breakthrough in the large-scale coordinated navigation of underwater unmanned systems."

The record-setting day could prove a major milestone in the PLA's development of autonomous drone swarms — an increasingly vital point of emphasis in the Eastern power's military buildup. Currently the world's largest navy, the PLA Navy (PLAN) is largely made up of smaller nimble ships designed to patrol the contested littoral waters of the South China Sea. And while the nation has commissioned three aircraft carriers in the last decade and a half, its fleet still lags behind the United States in total fire power.

Some believe drones could close this gap and enable PLA's increasingly expansive naval ambitions. Others worry the proliferation of drones will lower the threshold for naval engagements at a a time when naval intelligence is already beset with uncertainty.

The test's TH-618 drones resemble long pipes equipped with four propellers and a camera. Roughly 2 ft long and half a foot wide, the 11lb drones have a range of just over 18 miles and can operate continuously for roughly 8 hours. To navigate, the drones use smartphone cameras attached to the front, allowing AI systems to adjust routes in real time to respond to underwater disturbances and collisions. According to the university, the drones can autonomously conduct complex maneuvers like terrain tracking, landmark recognition, vertical lift, depth and speed control and hovering in water using multi-degree-of freedom thrusters.

Underwater drone navigation is exceedingly complex when compared to its aerial counterpart because developers cannot utilize wireless communications or satellite positioning and navigation systems for course calibration. Low visibility and high water pressure further complicate subsurface coordination. Drones must rely on an amalgamation of underwater sensing, subsurface target detection and acoustic communication to autonomously respond to their irregular operating environment. Coordinating these efforts is another challenge altogether.

The culmination of over two decades of research, this record-breaking exercise could represent a major leap in AI-powered underwater navigation technologies. Over roughly 34 minutes, the drones relied solely on visual data to autonomously navigate around the pool, covering over 1,600 feet without human intervention. In creating a miniaturized submersible capable of coordinating autonomous responses to environmental stimuli, researchers at NWPU may have taken a step towards a new era of subsurface research, intelligence gathering and warfare.

Of course, these technologies have a long way to go before they revolutionize China's naval operations. The controlled environment of a pool is a poor facsimile for proving the seaworthiness of autonomous submarine swarms. However, the test may indicate the direction of China's subsurface and drone development programs.

China has leaned heavily into subsurface vessels and research. For the PLA Navy, the investment is dual-purpose, potentially boosting the country's burgeoning mining and subsurface infrastructure efforts while enhancing its naval buildup and intelligence gathering. Both could have major geopolitical consequences given China's expansive military and economic ambitions.

Subsurface drone swarms could prove a major boon for Beijing's civilian and military naval operations. Researchers at Xi'an Tianhe Maritime Technologies highlighted the potential for coordinated swarms to monitor aquatic infrastructure and conduct research, such as inspecting gas pipelines or mapping the ocean floor. But this form of subsurface patrol could also lay the groundwork for submarine operations and defenses.

In March 2026, researchers at CNN, the Pulitzer Center and environmental outlet Mongabay reported that China had spent years surveying the maritime environments of strategically critical waterways. Billed by Beijing as exploration missions for deep-sea mining and oceanographic research, the effort extended far beyond Beijing's mining exploration rights and focused on key naval and economic shipping routes. Western military experts warned that the project likely boosted China's subsurface intelligence operations. With critical deep-sea research projects on the rise, China is likely to continue its multi-faceted push.

For the PLAN, these swarms are likely only part of a seabed-to-space defense architecture known as the Transparent Ocean. In July 2026, two PLA-affiliated research institutions published a proposal for teams of drones to patrol the contested waters of the South China Sea. The robotic "fortress" would see China patrolling its geopolitical neighborhood with swarms of aerial, surface and submarine drones.

Already, the PLAN has made major strides towards these goals. China is the first country to develop full-size uncrewed submarines. Moreover, its reportedly developing at least two submersible motherships to launch uncrewed subs and drone carriers across its aerial, surface and subsurface fleet.

China isn't alone in this pursuit. In the midst of the Russia-Ukraine war, in which a previously-inactive Ukrainian Navy downed one third of Russia's fleet through hoards of aerial, surface and submarine drones, global powers are racing to add these asymmetric weapons to their fleets.

Drones present a unique opportunity for countries without traditional naval capabilities. Extremely cheap and easy to build, militaries are learning to overwhelm opponents with these disposable attack vehicles. Thwarting such assaults is difficult, especially when the defense missiles cost millions more than their targets. Moreover, AI-coordinated drone swarms are an acute threat to aircraft carriers.

In December 2025, Ukraine orchestrated the first successful subsurface drone strike against an enemy submarine, hitting a Russian submarine in Novorossiysk. The development forced Russia to begin deploying anti-drone nets across its submarine fleet.

Traditional naval powers have made substantial progress towards deploying naval drones, too. Researchers from Poland, Germany, Portugal and Slovenia made strides towards launching autonomous subsurface drone swarms via the the European Defence Agency's Swarm of Biomimetic Underwater Vehicles project. The U.S. military acknowledged the dangers drones pose to its nuclear submarine fleet when it initiated an open call for submarine drone defense systems in July 2026. A few months later, the US Navy launched its first unmanned surface vessel alongside a carrier strike group.

But China may have an advantage over its rivals. As the world's leading manufacturer of both drone components and ships, its dual-purpose shipbuilding industrial complex is ideal for this type of military buildup. Unfortunately, the Trump administration won't be able to tariff its way out of this arms race.
